| A parameterized arithmetic subscript on a line that also has a command substitution or backtick is held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`); a direct substitution in an arithmetic subscript is read when its syntax is supported. | Bash may evaluate an expanded array index again, and the resulting command text may be unavailable to the parser. | Compute the value in a command of its own first, or approve the held call. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Arithmetic text supplied at run time by a variable set on another line, or by text the parser cannot read, can name a command bash runs while evaluating an array subscript; policy can't read that command. | The text is unavailable to the parser when the policy decision is made. | Keep destructive rights and secrets off the agent; use T2 or a sandbox for enforcement. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| `TK-D007` reads the shell `code`, which leaves out text an `echo`, `printf` or `cat`, or a `tee` whose output is redirected, only copies into a file when the rest of the call only lists, prints, changes folder or copies more text (`echo '…' > notes.md; ls`). A later call that runs or reads that file (`sh x.sh`) acts on what the text names unseen. Within one call the text is read (`cat > x.sh <<'EOF' … EOF` followed by `sh x.sh` is denied). | Each call is decided on its own: the rule can't follow text into a file and out of it in another call. | Rely on isolation (system mode, a sidecar or a central signer); `TK-D013` still denies a file tool's write to Tracekit's paths. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| `TK-A039`–`TK-A041` hold a destructive statement through a database client (`psql -c`, `mysql -e`, `mongosh`/`mongo --eval`, `sqlite3`'s bare SQL argument, `clickhouse-client --query`, `redis-cli`/`valkey-cli`, a wrapper's `docker exec`, `docker compose exec` or `kubectl exec`, an SQL-like MCP tool's `sql`/`query`/`statement` argument) but don't read one run from a file (`psql -f`, `mysql < file.sql`, a `mongosh` script file, a file piped into `redis-cli`), one piped or heredoc'd into `psql`, one built in a variable (`SQL="DROP TABLE x"; psql -c "$SQL"`), a Redis command sent through `EVAL` or a script, or a flush piped through a stage that isn't echo, printf or tee. `TK-A040` reads a flush echoed or printed into the client (also through `command`, `builtin` or `env`, by path, or in a `( )` or `{ }` group), fed to it as a herestring, or on a line of its own in a heredoc's first nine (also through any wrapper or `bash -c`); it misses one printed with other text (`printf '%s' FLUSHALL`, `echo -e 'SELECT 2\nFLUSHDB'`), `env` with options or assignments, a client another wrapper runs from a pipe (`echo FLUSHALL \| docker exec -i c redis-cli`), options after the herestring, more than eight options before it, and a heredoc's later lines. They also miss a statement after a delimiter of the client's own (`mysql --delimiter=//`), a `#` comment before the keyword, a block comment ending in `**/`, a backslash inside a `--` comment, in `TK-A041` a `--` comment containing `"`, `DROP\tTABLE` (an escaped tab) over MCP, an abbreviated option (`psql --comm`) and more than eight statements before the destructive one. A `psql`, `mysql` or `mariadb` short-option cluster of more than nine flags that take no value, ending in `c` or `e` (which no one needs), is held without being read; a value attached to an option that takes one (`psql -dmydatabase`, `mysql -pdevelopment`) is not such a cluster. A rolled-back destructive statement is held although the transaction would undo it: there is no ROLLBACK exemption, a comment or a mysql \G/\g terminator kept breaking it, so one approval for a test transaction is the price. | The rules read the command's words and the MCP tool's arguments as typed; a file's content and a variable's value aren't on them. They read `;` (and mysql's `\G`/`\g`) as a statement's end and `--` and `/* */` as comments, and skip at most eight statements, a bound that keeps the fallback engine fast. | Approve the held call, or run migrations through the deployment's reviewed migration path; keep production credentials out of the agent's reach. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A041` holds a destructive statement in an SQL-like MCP tool's `sql`/`query`/`statement` argument and reads the tool's name: only a tool whose name looks like a database tool is held, so an ordinary search whose `query` text is itself a destructive statement (a search for `DROP TABLE x`) is not. A call over 64 KiB to a tool whose name looks like a database tool (`execute_sql`, a bulk `insert_rows` on a `db` server) asks on `coding` and `dev` whatever it holds (`TK-OVERSIZE`), where any other MCP call that size is flagged: the rule reads the whole arguments, and a subject over 64 KiB is never cut. `server` and `strict` already deny every call that size. | SQL tool names are unbounded, so the rule matches names like `sql`, `postgres`, `mysql`, `sqlite`, `clickhouse`, `mongo` or `database`; a destructive statement through any other tool name is not held. | Approve the held call; the hold is ask, not deny. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| A shell whose language the parser doesn't read (`fish`, `csh`, `tcsh`, PowerShell, `nu`, `xonsh`, `elvish`) asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) whenever it runs commands from its line or stdin, even `pwsh -c 'Get-Date'`; a script file it runs (`fish build.fish`) isn't read, as a POSIX shell's isn't. | Their syntax isn't POSIX, so the rules anchored on a command's name can't read what they run. | Approve the held call, or run the command through a POSIX shell. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| With `on_parse_error: deny`, an ordinary command whose program the parser can't read (`$EDITOR notes.md`, a script piped into `bash`, an unclosed quote) is denied, not held. | The signer can't tell such a command from one built to hide what it runs, and with deny no person sees it. | Keep the default (`ask`) wherever a person answers holds; with deny, have the agent name programs literally. | [policy](policy-v2.md#file-format) |
| `strict.yaml` holds ordinary development work: every push, package install, `docker run`, network command and `PATH=`-style setting waits for a person (4.5% of E17's benign calls, 10.0% of E21's developer-session calls), and a command whose program comes from a variable (`$EDITOR notes.md`) is denied. | It trades interruptions for seeing every change that leaves the workspace before it runs; the flags it raises can't tell a routine push from a harmful one. | Use it where a person answers holds promptly; elsewhere use `server.yaml` and read the flags. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| On calls written blind to the rules (E21's held-out pool), the server pack stopped or held half the harmful ones on its one blind run (211 of 420), and 245 (109 denied) after rules were added that target the pool's shapes, a number that overstates blind recall; it holds 18 of 432 benign ones. It misses most exfiltration (27 of 39), privilege-escalation (19 of 27), prompt-injection (14 of 22), destructive (21 of 36) and defence-evasion (16 of 30) calls, and half the supply-chain ones (13 of 27). | The packs match known command shapes; calls phrased in ways no rule anticipates aren't matched. | Treat the packs as tripwires: run agents in a sandbox, keep credentials out of their reach, rely on the signer and witnesses for the record; `strict.yaml` holds more (274 of 420) at more interruptions. | [evaluation](evaluation.md#e21-policy-robustness) |
| A secret's value read in a way `TK-A035` to `TK-A038` don't name isn't held. They read the commands of AWS Secrets Manager and SSM Parameter Store, GCP Secret Manager and Azure Key Vault, and MCP tools whose names say they return a secret (or read an SSM parameter with decryption turned on). Not held: another store's shell commands (`vault kv get`, `op read`, `kubectl get secret`), a command run through a wrapper the shell parser doesn't read (`aws-vault exec … --`), an AWS call whose decryption comes from `--cli-input-json`, an ALL-CAPS tool name, an MCP tool whose server chose another name (`fetch_credential`) or a spelling the patterns lack, a CLI command given to an MCP tool whose name doesn't end in a word for running one (`TK-A036` reads `call_aws`, `run_command`, `execute`, `bash` and the like), an SDK call in an interpreter one-liner (`python -c`), and a credential that isn't in a secret store (`aws ecr get-login-password`, `aws sts assume-role`). A tool that both lists secrets and gets one (Azure's Key Vault tool) is held for both when its tool name says it gets a secret; one named only `keyvault` that takes the operation as an argument isn't. A call over 64 KiB to a tool `TK-A036` or `TK-A038` reads (`call_aws`, `run_command`, `get_parameter`) asks on `coding` and `dev` whatever it holds (`TK-OVERSIZE`), where any other MCP call that size is flagged: the rules read the whole arguments, and a subject over 64 KiB is never cut. `server` and `strict` already deny every call that size. | The rules read command words, tool names and the arguments the tool is given; they can't tell what a tool with another name returns. | Hold the tools your servers use for secrets by name in a rule of your own, and keep agents' IAM roles from reading secrets they don't need. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| A rule's `remedy` is advice the policy's author wrote: lint checks its form (one short present-tense sentence), not that it is right, and an agent may ignore it. | It is text shown with the decision, not a rule. | Write a remedy that names a safe alternative the agent can carry out; the deny or hold stands either way. | [policy](policy-v2.md#file-format) |
| A relative path, or one with `..`, is unverifiable; rules match its certain suffix. | A symlink could redirect it. | Write path rules that also work on suffixes. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Path rules, and shell rules on `target` and `effects`, are matched casefolded too (`cp x .ENV` is denied as `.env` is). | The agent's file system may ignore case whatever the signer's does. | Write path rules in lower case, or with both cases of a letter. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Patterns must stay inside an RE2 subset. | A decision must not depend on which engine is installed. | `tracekit policy lint` rejects the rest. | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| Without `google-re2`, a large subject, or a short one under a repeat of overlapping alternatives (`secret(.\|\s)*key`), can exhaust the 1 s budget: the decision denies, is marked `nondeterministic: true` and says so in its reason, so the two engines can decide differently. Every shipped pattern searches 64 KiB built to make it back off or rescan within 150 ms on CI's runners (about 20 ms on a laptop), except eight on one command or SQL statement that repeats their first word thousands of times, measured at 64 KiB on an M-series Mac: `TK-D002`, `TK-D004`, `TK-D006`, `TK-D011` and `TK-DB003` over 5 s, `TK-C010` 3.4 s, `TK-D007` 0.3 s and `TK-A032` 0.05 s (`curl` with no pipe, `rm -r` with no root or home path, `curl` with no `.env` upload, `cat` with no upload, `DELETE FROM` with no `;`, `aws s3 rm` with no `--recursive`, `kill` with no `tracekit` after it, `aws s3 sync` whose `s3://` words are each followed by an option and none ends the command): each occurrence rescans the rest, so such a call can deny on the fallback engine where RE2 decides it. `TK-A039` reads up to sixteen words after `sqlite3` as options before its SQL, so 64 KiB of short words after `sqlite3` takes about 0.2 s, and `TK-A040` up to eight options after each `redis-cli` on a line, so 64 KiB of `redis-cli -a ` takes about 0.07 s on a laptop and up to 0.2 s on CI's runners (both linear); `TK-A029` takes about 0.1 s on 64 KiB of newlines after `journalctl`, as 1.4.2 did. | The fallback `regex` engine backtracks; RE2 is linear. A rule that looks for one thing after another with any text between can't be written linearly for a backtracking engine without atomic groups, which RE2 lacks. | Install `google-re2` on the signer (a dependency from Python 3.10). | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| `TK-F007` flags a detached tmux session only when its `-d` comes within 200 characters after `tmux new`, on either engine; one further along isn't flagged. | The bound keeps the pattern linear on the fallback engine, and both engines run the same pattern so they decide alike. | Read long `tmux new` commands in the record yourself, or add a rule of your own for the tmux options your agents use. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `tracekit policy lint` refuses an unbounded repeat whose body starts with an unbounded repeat (`(a+)+`), not every super-linear pattern: a bounded repeat of one (`^(.*?,){11}P`: 5 s on 260 characters) or overlapping alternatives under a repeat (`(a\|aa)+$`: 3 s on 35) pass it. | Refusing every repeat over an unbounded body would refuse linear patterns too (`TK-M003`'s `([^"@]*@){10}`); telling them apart needs to know whether the repeated class can match what follows it. | Install `google-re2`; time a new pattern on the fallback engine before shipping it (the speed test in `tests/test_policy2.py` does this for the shipped packs). | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| A rule's subject over 64 KiB is never cut: the rule's own section decides. | Cutting or windowing would let content hide past the cut. | Expect large arguments to be flagged, held or denied by the matching section. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |
| A `decide` whose parsed arguments nest deeper than a request may (128 levels, counting the request's own, or too deep to check) is refused as a whole (`invalid_request`), not decided: no `policy.decision` names the call, only the signer's refusal count does. Raw arguments (`args_source: "raw"`) nested past 128 levels are denied and recorded (`TK-ARGS-INVALID`). | Checking, hashing or copying such a request would exceed the parser's limit before anything could be recorded. | Nothing to do for safety: the hooks and integrations don't run a call they couldn't get decided. Flatten arguments that deep if a tool really needs them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |

| In a shell command, a word that reads as an IPv4 address in any notation (`2852039166`, `169.16689662`) is a target URL rules match, and so is a dotted number such as a version (`npm version 1.2.3`): `TK-F012` flags it as egress. | The signer can't tell a version from an address without knowing every command's arguments. | Read `egress` flags on version-like words as noise; an allowlist of hosts goes in a rule's `unless`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A browser rule on a click matches the page the click ran on, not the request it makes: navigating straight to the URL a denied button submits to, or running page JavaScript that submits the form, is another action. | A click's arguments name an element, not where it leads. | Deny the state-changing URL on navigations too (`class: browser`, `field: url`); keep `TK-B012` holding `evaluate`. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#the-browser-policy-pack) |
| The server pack's allowlists (`TK-N002`, `TK-B011`) name `example.com` until you redefine them. | Allowlists are deployment-specific. | Redefine those rules by id in your policy. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| Parsed arguments that hold a lone surrogate (text typed or read from a broken page) are a signed deny, `TK-ARGS-INVALID`, not a decision on their content. | JSON can't carry a lone surrogate, so the client sends them as raw text, which isn't strict JSON. | Clean the text before the call, or expect the deny. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |
| Cloud-SDK-shaped tools other than CLIs and MCP tools aren't covered by the cloud pack. | Their argument shapes vary. | Map them under `tools` and add rules of your own. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |

| `TK-A022` and `TK-A023` cover writes to Docker daemon configuration/socket paths and direct `docker run` or `podman run` flags for privileged mode, host PID space or root mounts. `docker container run` and mounts of the Docker socket are not covered; compose contents, other runtimes, remote daemons and API calls aren't inspected either. | File policy sees the path, not file contents; shell policy sees only command arguments. | Review compose changes and remote/container API access separately, or use a runtime policy that sees the resolved container configuration. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D021` blocks direct HTTP(S) package URLs in named install commands; `TK-A028` asks on version-control URLs. `npm i <url>`, `pip install -q <url>`, `pip install -U <url>`, other option orders, preconfigured unknown sources, transitive dependency URLs and other package clients may not match. `TK-A024` and `TK-A030` hold a file tool's or a shell command's write to known source-config paths (`.npmrc`, `pip.conf`, `.pypirc`, apt sources, yum repos), and `TK-A025` installs with explicit non-default index, registry or `--find-links` options (pip, uv, uvx, npm, npx, pnpm, yarn, bun, poetry, cargo, gem), `config set` (`npm set`) of the index or registry, and the source variables pip, uv and npm read set before a command or through `env` or `export` (`PIP_INDEX_URL`, `UV_INDEX_URL`, `npm_config_registry`, …). An index named inside a requirements or lock file (`pip install -r` of a file with `--index-url`), a config file chosen by a variable (`PIP_CONFIG_FILE`, `NPM_CONFIG_USERCONFIG`), a config file written by an interpreter or under another name, and other package managers (conda, `poetry source add`) aren't held. | The signer doesn't resolve package-manager configuration or dependency metadata before deciding, and reads files only by the names a command gives them. | Pin and verify package sources in a controlled build environment. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A026` and `TK-A027` hold edits and recognized shell writes/deletes (`effects`: a redirection, `rm`, `mv`, `cp`, `ln`, `tee`, `truncate`, `shred`, `dd`, `sed -i`, `perl -i`, `find -delete`) to named history and log paths, and `TK-A029` `history -c`, turning history off and vacuuming the journal. Other history-file writes such as `history -w`, application-managed history, remote logs and paths reached through an unrecognized program (`python -c`) may not match. | The rules match visible paths and command arguments; they don't observe filesystem effects. | Preserve logs outside the agent's control and use a sandbox or kernel sensor to detect writes. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A027` holds `find … -delete` and `find … -exec rm` (or `unlink`, `shred`, `truncate`) on history and log paths, not a delete made by a shell `find` runs (`find /var/log -exec sh -c 'rm "$1"' _ {} \;`): the paths reach that `rm` only as `$1`. | Variables aren't expanded, so the rule can't tell which files the inner shell is given. | Add an `ask` rule of your own for `find` with `-exec sh` on the paths that matter, or keep logs outside the agent's reach. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D008` (the harness's transcripts), `TK-A027` (history and logs), `TK-A030` (package sources) and `TK-F015` (harness settings) read a relative write or delete target as it is typed, not from the folder an earlier `cd` or `pushd` moved into: `cd ~/.codex/sessions && rm *.jsonl`, `cd ~/.claude/projects/x && : > s.jsonl` and `cd ~/.codex && rm -rf sessions` aren't denied, `cd /var/log && rm syslog` and `cd /etc/apt/sources.list.d && echo deb x > evil.list` aren't held, and `cd ~/.claude && echo '{}' > settings.json` isn't flagged. A relative target that still names the folder is matched (`cd ~ && rm -rf .claude/projects` is denied). | The shell parser doesn't follow `cd`: which folder a relative path lands in is known only when the command runs. | Run the agent in a sandbox that can't write outside its workspace ([Claude Code's](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md), [Gemini CLI's](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md)), and on Linux import a kernel sensor's records ([Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md)): each file write is signed into the run with its path, and `tracekit analyze --v2` flags one no call explains (`TK-X206`). | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A024` and `TK-A025` read npm's and pip's ways of setting a package source; Yarn Berry's (`yarn config set npmRegistryServer <url>`, or `.yarnrc.yml` written by a file tool) isn't held. | Each package manager names its registry setting differently, and the rules name npm's, pip's, apt's and yum's. | If your agents use Yarn 2 or later, add `ask` rules of your own for `yarn config set npmRegistryServer` and writes to `.yarnrc.yml`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| The `payment` class doesn't extract `new_payee` yet. | Not built. | Match payees with your own rules. | [policy](policy-v2.md#classes-and-fields) |
| `unless` on a whole command line could exempt a different target. | The subject holds several targets. | Use `unless` only where the subject is one target. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| v1 rules (`tracekit/policy/default.yaml`) match raw strings, so quoted text that mentions a risky command can trip them. | The v1 engine doesn't parse commands. | Use the v2 signer's policy; add exceptions for known false positives. | [evaluation](evaluation.md#e3-policy-gate) |

## Provenance (1.1)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.1)** Provenance matches values verbatim: a value the model paraphrases, splits or re-encodes reads as a new value. | It is a tripwire on the commonest way injected instructions act, not information-flow control. | To prevent the flow, use information-flow control (CaMeL, FIDES) in the agent. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Only untrusted tools' results and observed inputs are indexed: a page fetched with a tool the policy doesn't list as untrusted (`curl` in a shell) isn't seen. | Results of other tools can echo the agent's own arguments, so they aren't counted either way. | List such tools under `untrusted`, or observe the content with `run.observe`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Only an input observed as trusted makes a value trusted: a value from your own data that also appears in untrusted content (a customer's address from the CRM that is also in their ticket) is held. | Counting other tool output as trusted could launder an injected value. | Observe the record you looked up as `trusted`, or answer the hold. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Whenever a run's index is not `complete`, `from: untrusted` rules fail closed: they match whenever their pattern does (the packs' `TK-P` rules ask). It is `unavailable` after a signer restart, `truncated` once a result carries more than 4096 values (or 1 MiB of text) or the run reaches 20,000, `partial` once an untrusted tool completes with a digest or a client's events skip a `client_seq` (an observe or a result lost in a signer outage), and `timeout` for a decision whose values took past its 1 s deadline. | The index lives in memory only and is bounded; a value it can't vouch for may be injected, and a lost event may have carried one. | Watch `provenance_state` and `provenance_reason` on decisions; split very long runs; expect more holds after a restart or an outage, and start the task again in a new run to end them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Provenance needs the result itself: for a tool the policy lists as untrusted, `decide` answers `send_result` and the shipped adapters (Python, TypeScript, the Claude Code hook) send the result instead of its digest. A result over 512 KiB, or an adapter of your own that sends a digest, makes the run `partial`. | The signer can only index content it receives; it redacts and commits to it and keeps nothing in clear. | In your own adapter, send `result` itself when the decision says `send_result`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted), [privacy](privacy.md) |
| **(1.1)** `trust: "trusted"` on `observe` is the calling process's claim. Provenance protects against injected content, not against a malicious agent program, which can observe anything as trusted (or send digests) and get past `from` rules. | The signer can't tell who wrote content the caller supplies. | Observe the task from your application, not from the agent; use the other rules for a hostile agent. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Bare IPv6 addresses and integer IPs in text are not read as hosts (inside a URL they are). | Bare forms are ambiguous in prose. | Rely on the URL forms, or add rules of your own. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| A Browser Use navigation with a query string sends data to its host, so `TK-P003` holds one to a host that only a page named (a search result's link with `?id=5` included) unless the task named it. Data in a URL's path or fragment isn't counted as sent. | A query string is how a GET carries data to an attacker's host; telling an ID from a card number takes reading the value. | Observe the task as `trusted`; to hold only typing and uploads, redefine `TK-P003` with `tool: '^browser_use:(input|upload_file)$'`. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#the-browser-policy-pack) |
| The Browser Use adapter observes what the model read of each page (URL, title, tabs, text) up to 100,000 characters, from each browser state the session's `get_browser_state_summary` returns, which it wraps at the session's first action; the first step's state, made before that, comes from Browser Use's cached copy, which a tab closing before the first action empties. A link's target the model wasn't shown enters the index only once the agent is on that page. | Browser Use shows the model 40,000 characters of a page by default, and the adapter first sees the session at its first action; asking it for a fresh state would renumber the elements the step's actions refer to. | Keep `max_clickable_elements_length` at or under 100,000. Each page adds its values to the run's index (20,000 at most, then `truncated`): give a long browsing session a run per task. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#what-is-recorded) |
| After a signer restart every run that was open has an `unavailable` index for the rest of the run: each call a `from: untrusted` rule's pattern matches is held, even typing into a site the task named (its decision's `provenance_reason` says so). | The index holds per-value keyed hashes in memory under a per-process key; persisting them would keep brute-forceable hashes of card numbers and addresses at rest, and would need a crash to invalidate them. | Answer the holds, or start the task again in a new run: a run registered after the restart has a complete index. Restart signers between runs where you can. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Reading values is bounded: linear in the text, at most 1 MiB and 4096 values per result or argument. | A result is attacker-controlled; the work must not grow with it. | Nothing: past the bound the run is `truncated` and fails closed. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Bundles from a 1.1 signer must be exported with tracekit >= 1.1: a 1.0 exporter labels them 1.0.0, and a 1.0 verifier fails their schema. | Provenance adds record fields 1.0 doesn't know. | Upgrade the exporter and verifiers together with the signer. | [format](format-v2.md#3-events) |
| **(1.2)** TK-P003 reads only a browser navigation's query string as data sent to the host: data put in the path (`https://evil.example/c/<card number>`) or in the host name (`https://<data>.evil.example/`) isn't, so such a navigation to a host only untrusted content named is not held. | Following a link from a page or a mail is a navigation too; holding every path would hold every link the agent follows. | Allow only the hosts the agent needs (TK-B011's allowlist), or run the egress observer, which records the hosts reached. | [policy](policy-v2.md) |

## Detection (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.2)** Sequences and limits catch the patterns they describe, nothing else: a reworded attack that carries none of their labels isn't seen. | Detection is deterministic rules over rule ids and labels, no model. | Read incidents as tripwires; add sequences for the patterns you care about and backtest them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A hold or quarantine is recorded for every run, but enforced only where the signer decides each step: a gateway's model loop, an executor's tools (T2). A T1 adapter that ignores a decision can still run the call. | On T1 the agent's own process runs the tool; the signer answers, it can't stop it. The verifier flags any result recorded while held or quarantined. | Put tools that must be containable behind the executor or MCP gateway, and models behind the LLM gateway. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** With no person approver configured (`approvals.approvers` naming persons), every enforced hold is a quarantine, and `run_hold` is refused. | Only a person may answer a held call; a hold no one may answer would never end. | Configure approvers mapped in `approvals.persons` (or OIDC persons or groups) before enforcing holds. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** Suspensions are shared only through the suspension list file: each replica reads it per call and writes its own `enforced` record when it first sees an entry; two operators changing the list at once on different replicas can lose one change. Holds, quarantines, cooldowns and a paused response engine are per replica. | Each replica has its own log and state; the list is the one thing they share, and no token is revoked automatically. | Put the list on storage every replica reads (`suspensions:`), change it from one place, and send pause and resume to every replica (`tracekit signer incidents pause --signer ... --signer ...`). | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** `tracekit signer incidents list` reaches only the replicas you name with `--signer` (the default: one). | Incidents live in each replica's log; there is no central index. | Name every replica's URL when listing, or read incidents from each log's bundle. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** After a restart that loses the detector's state, a decision whose detection state is `unavailable` holds its run (`detector_unavailable`) when the policy enforces a hold or quarantine. | Fail closed: the detector can't vouch that no sequence completed. | Keep snapshots (they hold the detector's state); expect held runs to resume by a person after an unclean restart. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The call that completes a sequence was decided `allow` and counted before the hold: it is decided again, held, so it doesn't run on that decision; a call consumed by a person's approval as it fires a hold stays in flight. | Detection reads the signed decision, so the decision exists before the incident. | Read `in_flight` on `incident.opened`: those calls may still complete. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The verifier recomputes only incidents of scope `run` whose cites are in their run, under the one policy that run was decided by; session, owner and principal incidents, operator incidents and runs decided under several policies are counted signer-asserted. | A bundle holds one run's records; other runs' records and earlier policies aren't in it. | Export with the signer's config so its policies are bundled; export the run-set to see the other runs of a scope. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** Incident webhooks are delivered at least once (a receiver may see one twice) and a 4xx-refusing endpoint is skipped. A new webhook starts at the log's end, not its beginning; one whose saved cursor is unreadable sends every incident record of the log again. | The outbox is a cursor over the log that moves only once the endpoint took a batch; a refusal won't change by resending. | Deduplicate by incident id and record uid; fix the endpoint and read missed incidents from the log or `tracekit signer incidents list`. | [webhooks](webhooks.md) |
| **(1.2)** No trigger suspends an identity: `respond` holds or quarantines runs, and only an operator suspends (lint refuses `suspend:` in `respond`). | A run's owner is often an identity every conversation of a server shares, and content the agent reads can steer every trigger, so an automatic suspension could stop every user's agent. | Alert on incidents (`action: notify`) and suspend with `tracekit signer incidents suspend` after a look. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The signer refuses to start when one `authorize` entry grants both `register_run` and a containment operator call. | An agent's identity must not resolve, resume or pause its own containment. | Give operators an identity of their own (a person's OIDC login or a separate uid or token). | [incidents](incidents.md#operators) |
| **(1.2)** `session` scope joins runs only through `parent_run`: a sequence split across runs registered without `parent` is two sessions. | The signer joins runs only on a link it can check (the parent's run token). | Register subagent runs with `parent`; use `owner` scope to join all runs of one identity. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Windows use the signer's clock, and are fixed: an NTP step moves them, and a burst straddling two windows can reach up to twice `max` before a limit fires. | Records' `ts` is the only clock a verifier can recompute; fixed windows keep the work O(1) per call. | Keep the signer on NTP; set `max` at half the burst you must catch. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A sum limit holds a call of a known class it counts without a non-negative integer amount and a three-letter currency code (`ask`, `TK-LIMIT-SUM`), unless its `respond` mode is `off`; approved, or of an unknown class, such a call isn't added to the total. | A total across currencies, of negative amounts or of an unreadable amount would mean nothing; an unmapped tool's arguments needn't be a payment's. | Send amounts as non-negative integers in minor units with their currency code; map payment tools to a class; approve held calls knowing the limit doesn't see them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Decisions say `detect.state: unavailable`, and steps and counts from before are missed, after a restart from a snapshot written without detection or under another policy, after a replay of decisions made under another policy, and for a run the detector never saw registered. | What came before such a snapshot is no longer read, and another policy's decisions carry its rule ids, not this one's. | Change a detection policy when runs are quiet; expect missed steps and counts until the longest window has passed after the restart or the last such decision, and backtest the store for them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A scope with no record for an hour (or the policy's longest window, if longer) starts over, open incidents included: a later match opens a new one. | State must not grow with every run and owner the signer has seen, and the reset follows the records' `ts`, so a backtest or verifier gets the same incidents. | Group incidents by trigger and scope when you read them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Only opened incidents count toward the caps: past 100 of one trigger in a run, 20 from one run in a minute, or 60 in a tenant in a minute, one `incident.capped` is written and the rest are not. An open incident is updated at most once per window of its trigger (a minute for one without). | A loop must not flood the log with incidents, and one run or trigger must not use up another's. The share is per run, so an identity with several runs can use up more of the tenant's 60, and another agent's incident that minute is only `incident.capped`. | Read the run's decisions (their `detect` field) or backtest the policy to see the rest; keep `open_runs` quotas tight for agent identities. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** The server pack's limits were checked on small shipped runs only, and `TK-Q001`'s window was not backtested. | Those are the runs that ship, and none of them reads a secret or sends anything. | Run the pack in shadow, backtest your own store (`tracekit policy backtest`) and set `max` above your busiest minute. | [policy](policy-v2.md#choosing-thresholds) |
| **(1.2)** One call whose own names match consecutive steps takes them all, each name once (a command that reads a secret and sends it completes `[secrets_access, egress]`); a step matched only through `*` or an unknown class takes a call one step. | An unmapped tool matches every step; letting it chain would complete any sequence in one call. | Give each step its own label; a sequence that repeats a label needs that many calls. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** `egress` (`TK-F012`, `TK-F013`) is a URL a call acts on or a network command; `secrets_access` (`TK-F005`) is a call that acts on a secrets file's name, writes included (a lone env template path, such as `.env.example`, excepted). Mail, payments, SQL, a program that opens its own connection, and content that holds a secret without such a name are neither. | The labels read what the signer can see in the arguments, deterministically. | Label your own tools that send data or read secrets (`label: egress` / `secrets_access` on a flag rule) to bring them into `TK-Q001`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A step counts when its call is decided (`allow`, `flag`) or its approval consumed, whether or not the call then runs; a call that runs after a deny or an unapproved ask never counts as a step. | Detection reads decisions, which the signer has before a call runs; a call run against its decision is `executed_against_policy`. | Keep the server pack's `executed_against_policy` trigger on (shadow, notify), and read those incidents alongside sequences. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A decision retried under a new `request_id` is a new decision and counts again. | Each decide writes its own signed decision; remembering every counted call would grow the detector's state with calls. It can only over-count. | Retry with the same `request_id` (the signer answers it from its cache); allow for client retries when you set `max`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** An incident of a `session`, `owner` or `principal` scope is opened in the run whose record fired it; its `incident.updated` records are written in the runs whose records matched later. | Every incident record is written in the run of the record that fired it, so it lands in that run's hash chain. | Follow an incident by its `incident_id` across the runs of its scope. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** `backtest` reads the rule ids decisions recorded: for decisions made under another policy, rules and labels the new one adds or changes are not applied. | Arguments are not stored, so a decision can't be made again. | Heed its warning (the count of such decisions); backtest changes to sequences, limits and `respond`, and measure new rules by running the policy in shadow. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A bundle with `detect` fields or incident records needs a 1.2 verifier. | They are format 1.2 additions. | Upgrade verifiers with the signer. | [format](format-v2.md#3-events) |

## Content vault (1.1)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.1)** With `content.retain` on, whoever holds `keys/content.key` and the store can read retained content. | The signer must decrypt it to answer `content_get`. | Keep the keys dir to the signer's user (`D-CONTENT-VAULT`), keep retention short, erase runs you no longer need. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** The content master key is a file key (`keys/content.key`), not wrapped by KMS, even when the log key is in AWS KMS. | The KMS log key is an Ed25519 signing key, which can't wrap keys. | Protect the data volume as you do the other file keys; back the key up with them. | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** `keys/content.key` has no rotation path: replacing it makes every run already in the vault unreadable (`content_get` answers `unavailable` for them). | Each run's key is wrapped by the master key, and nothing re-wraps them. | Keep the key for as long as you keep content; replace it only to give up everything the vault holds. | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** Erasure (and retention) reaches the live store only: a backup, WAL archive or replica taken earlier still holds the run's wrapped key and blobs, and opens with `keys/content.key`. | The signer can't delete copies it doesn't hold. | Keep backups and archives shorter than `retention_days`, or rotate `content.key` to cut off older copies, which today also makes every run still in the vault unreadable (no re-wrapping yet). | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** A decision's arguments are kept redacted: if redaction changed them, they no longer open `args_commitment` (`content_get` marks them `redacted`, without a salt). The same holds for a model exchange redaction changed whose tool uses carry `args_commitment`: no salt, so its `content_digest` can't be checked either. | Nothing unredacted is written; the policy decided on the unredacted arguments. | Use the tool result and the model exchange, which open their commitments, for what the call did. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** Content over `max_record_bytes` isn't kept (`too_large`), and one `content_get` answer must fit the 1 MiB frame, so an item near 1 MiB can't be read. | The RPC answers in one frame. | Ask for fewer seqs at a time (the refusal says so); keep `max_record_bytes` well under 1 MiB if you need every item back. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** The gateway asks the signer once whether the vault is on, needs the `status` grant, and sends no body over half a frame (`not_retained`). | One status call per gateway process; the frame bounds a request. | Grant the gateway `status`; restart it after turning the vault on or off. | [gateway](gateway.md#configure) |
| **(1.1)** Erasing a run erases the replay runs (`tracekit why`) the vault holds for it when it runs; a replay run that is still being written as the run is erased can keep what it records after that. Finding them reads the first record of every run with content in the vault. | Replay runs copy the run's content into their own requests; the store does not index runs by what they replay. | Erase a run once no investigation of it is running. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.2.2)** The dev signer keeps each call's redacted arguments and results for 7 days in its data dir, readable by your user, so the dev viewer can show them. | The viewer needs the content, and the dev signer runs as you: you hold every dev key already. | `TRACEKIT_DEV_CONTENT=0` before the dev signer starts keeps nothing (the viewer then shows rule reasons only). | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** Retention counts from the first item kept for a run, not per record. | A run's items share one key, erased at once. | Pick `retention_days` for your longest runs. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** `content_get` and `content_erase` reach runs of the caller's own tenant only. | The tenant comes from the caller's identity. | Give each tenant's investigator its own identity. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** On an existing Postgres database, `tracekit signer migrate` adds the vault's tables but grants the signer's role nothing on them: until you do, items read back `not_retained`. | `migrate` runs the schema only; grants are applied by the init script or by hand. | Apply `tracekit.storage.postgres.GRANTS` to the signer's role after migrating (the signer's log line says so). | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** An item is stored just after its record: a crash or storage error in between leaves it `not_retained`, and a model body that isn't strict JSON (an integer past 2^53, a non-finite number, a lone surrogate) is recorded without it (the gateway sends none: `not_retained`). | The record is never held back for its content. | Treat `not_retained` as missing evidence, not as an empty value. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |

## Approvals

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| On the dev signer (no `signer.yaml`) the requester can answer its own approval. A signer started from `signer.yaml` refuses that unless `approvals: {self_approval: allow}`, and without an `approvals` section has no approvers at all. The shipped packs deny the agent's own `tracekit approve` or `reject` and `tracekit approvals approve` or `reject` (`TK-D012`), also through the wrappers and runners the shell parser reads (`env`, `sh -c`, `eval`, `uvx`, `uv tool run`, `uv run`, `pipx run`, `hatch run`, `conda run`, …) and as `python -m tracekit`. They don't deny code that calls the signer's `approval_decide` itself (`python -c`, a script), a name or action that comes at run time (`xargs tracekit` reading it from stdin, `sh -c 'tracekit "$@"'`), a runner's own options before its subcommand (`uv -q run`), a short-option cluster that ends in an option taking a value (`uvx -qc c.txt tracekit`), or a runner the parser doesn't name (`mamba run`, `hatch env run`). | The dev signer has no separate approver; a policy rule reads commands, not what a program does. | Configure `approvals: {approvers: [...]}` (system mode does, and refuses the agent's own answer); self-approved answers cap assurance at `dev` and show `approvals: self`. | [approvals](approvals.md#who-may-answer) |
| A T1 approval binds the arguments the agent reported, and the agent's process runs the call. | The adapter runs in the agent. | Use `approval: {executor: t2}` and route the call through the [tool executor](executor.md) or the [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md), which run the approved arguments outside the agent. Adapters run the approved arguments too, but in the agent's process. | [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |
| Approvers answering through the web viewer or Slack are vouched for by the bridge (`attested: false`). | The signer authenticates the bridge, not the person. | Grant `approval_decide_on_behalf` only to bridges you run; read `via` on each approval. | [approvals](approvals.md#web-approvals-and-passkeys), [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| The signer takes the bridge's word for who registers a passkey, and keeps a person's first passkey. | It has no proof of its own of the person behind a session. | Have each approver register right after onboarding; check `passkeys.json` against the approver list. | [approvals](approvals.md#web-approvals-and-passkeys) |
| Break-glass answers aren't available through Slack. | Break-glass needs a reason and a direct identity. | Answer break-glass approvals from the CLI. | [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| After a restart the Slack bridge posts the approvals still pending again. | It keeps which messages it posted in memory. | Expect duplicate messages after a restart. | [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| An approval expires one hour after it was requested. | Nothing should consume a stale approval. | Answer within the hour, or request again. | [approvals](approvals.md#expiry-and-abandon) |
| An MCP call is named by the server's own name (`mcp:<server>/<tool>`); an mcp 1.x session whose `initialize()` ran before `tracekit.instrument()`, or one wrapped by hand without `server=`, is recorded as `mcp:unknown/<tool>`, with a warning. | The name comes from the server's initialize result, which an mcp 1.x session doesn't keep. | Call `tracekit.instrument()` before any session initializes, or pass `TracekitSession(..., server="<name>")`; write rules for server-named tools knowing the server picks its name. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered) |
| An adapter without saved state (MCP, Browser Use, the Claude Agent SDK hook) holds a call while waiting; the wait doesn't survive the process, and a long wait ends in a denial. | There is no resumable state to carry the approval. | Answer within the adapter's wait; use a framework with saved state for long waits. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered), [Claude Agent SDK](integrations/claude-agent-sdk.md#not-covered) |
| LangGraph: code that catches exceptions around a gated `ToolNode` (a `try`/`except Exception` in a node that invokes it, a middleware outside `TracekitMiddleware` that turns exceptions into messages) ends an `ask`'s pause: the model gets an error, the call doesn't run, and its approval expires. The node's own `handle_tool_errors` and `.with_fallbacks()` let the pause through. | LangGraph pauses by raising `GraphInterrupt` out of the node; code that catches it ends the pause before LangGraph sees it. | Re-raise `langgraph.errors.GraphBubbleUp` before any broad `except`, or put the error handling in the ToolNode (`handle_tool_errors`, `.with_fallbacks()`). | [LangGraph](quickstarts/langchain.md#approvals-wire-it-by-hand) |
| A Browser Use action waits for an approval at most five sixths of Browser Use's action timeout (150 s of 180 s); then the approval is withdrawn and the action refused. | Browser Use cancels an action that outlives its timeout, approval wait included, with a misleading "dead CDP WebSocket" error. | Answer within the wait, or raise `BROWSER_USE_ACTION_TIMEOUT_S` (and the Agent's `step_timeout`) to give people longer. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#decisions) |
| OpenAI Agents' `LocalShellTool` and `ApplyPatchTool` can't wait for an approval: an `ask` for them is refused. | The SDK gives no approval hook for them. | Allow or deny them by policy. | [OpenAI Agents](integrations/openai-agents.md#not-covered) |
| v1 approvals name the held call, not its exact arguments. | v1 approvals predate argument binding. | Use the v2 signer, which binds approvals to the exact arguments. | [laptop threat model](threat-model-laptop.md#open-problems) |
| **(1.2)** The agent-approvals kill switch (`approval_agents`) is signer-wide: switching it off stops and revokes agent approvals for every tenant. | It protects the signer from a misbehaving reviewer, which may serve several tenants. | Grant `approval_agents` only to the signer's operators, not to one tenant's. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** An agent approver's bounds bind the approval, not the execution, unless the rule has `executor: t2`. | Under T1 the agent's own process runs the call with the arguments it holds. | Put `executor: t2` on agent rules, above all on money-moving ones (`tracekit policy lint` warns), and run held calls in a worker that executes what `approval_consume` returns. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** A reviewer agent's judgment is model output: within the bounds, the requesting agent can steer it. | It reads arguments the requesting agent chose. | Set bounds you would accept every call inside of; start in `mode: shadow` and compare its suggestions with your people's answers. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The untrusted-value floor relies on an honest adapter's trust labels: content the adapter reports as trusted (or never reports) does not send a call to people. | `observe`'s `trust` is the caller's claim, and only untrusted tools' results are indexed. | List every tool whose results come from outside in the policy's `untrusted`, and observe outside content as untrusted. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.2)** Budgets and review caps are counted per signer: replicas that each write their own log multiply them. | Each signer counts the agent approvals in its own records. | Divide budgets by the number of replicas, or route a tenant's runs to one replica (`route`). | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** An agent approval under a `sum` budget records the value it took (`spent`) in the clear. | The budget is rebuilt from the signed records, which otherwise hold commitments only. | Use `sum` on amounts you are fine to see in a bundle; use `count` alone otherwise. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** Anti-shopping matches the same arguments or the same tool call id: a call with one argument changed is a new call an agent may answer. | Any other match would need a notion of "near" arguments. | Rely on the bounds and budgets; a run gets at most 10 agent reviews and a tenant 1000 an hour, then people answer. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** After a signer restart, calls of runs registered before it go to people. | The provenance index lives in memory, so their provenance is `unavailable`. | Expect more person answers right after a restart; new runs are not affected. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The verifiers can't check an agent approval's argument bounds. | The records hold the arguments only as salted commitments. | Read the `approvals by agents` line as "bounds the signer checked"; to audit one, `tracekit signer reveal --record <seq>` and recompute its commitment from the arguments. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#approvals-in-the-report), [privacy](privacy.md#the-v2-signer) |
| **(1.2)** Without the policy snapshot of the decision's `policy_hash` in the bundle, the verifiers don't check that the rules permitted agents (a warning: `agent approval bounds unchecked`). | `tracekit export --v2` bundles the policy an agent approval's decision cites from the signer's `<data_dir>/policies`, where the signer writes each policy it starts with; an export with another data dir (another replica, a copied store) or after that file was removed has none. | Export with the signer's config; keep `<data_dir>/policies` with the store, or check the old policy file's hash by hand. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#approvals-in-the-report) |
| **(1.2)** An approval an agent's answer can decide (enforce mode) reaches people (Slack, the desk's "for people") only once it is escalated: with no agent answering, after `agent_timeout_s` (default 600 s). | The signer gives the agent approver that long to answer first. | Keep the reviewer running on every replica; lower `approvals.agent_timeout_s` for faster handover; `approval_agents {enabled: false}` hands every pending one to people at once. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The reference reviewer sends the redacted arguments of the tenants it serves to a model provider. | Judging a call means reading it. | List in its `tenants` only tenants that accept this; see [privacy](privacy.md#what-leaves-the-machine). | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** The reference reviewer keeps which approvals it answered in memory: after a restart it answers a shadow-mode approval still pending again (a second `approval.suggested`). | Shadow answers leave the approval listed for agents. | Count suggestions per approval id when comparing; enforce-mode answers are not affected (the signer refuses a second one). | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** `tracekit reviewer eval` judges views without arguments. | Past arguments are in no record (salted commitments) and the signer deletes its copy once an approval ends. | Treat eval as a first check of the prompt and the rules; measure on live traffic with `mode: shadow`, where the view is complete. | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** Each review registers a run of its own on the signer (the reviewer's run identity), one per approval. | The model call is evidence too, linked to the approval it judged. | Size the signer's run quotas for one extra run per agent-answered approval. | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** An agent's approval runs only within `agent_consume_s` (and after `veto_s`); an adapter that waits longer gets `TK-APPROVAL-EXPIRED`. | An unconsumed agent approval should not wait for a better moment. | Consume right after approval; raise `approvals.agent_consume_s` for slow adapters. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The verifiers check who answered an approval and that an agent's answer stays within its bounds, not the order of the approval's records: an agent answer after `approval.expired` or after another agent's rejection, a consume after `approval.revoked`, or a consume outside `not_before`/`consume_by` still verifies. | The signer enforces each of these when it happens; the offline check of their order is not built yet. | Read an approval's records in order (`tracekit approvals show`, the viewer) when a veto or an expiry matters. | [approvals](approvals.md) |

## Custody (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.2)** An approval the executor or MCP gateway stopped waiting for stays `requested` until it expires (an hour): a person may still approve it, and it runs only if the agent sends the same `tool_call_id` again. | The gateway holds the call, and `approval_abandon` is the agent's call, which may not touch a held one. | Reject approvals whose caller has given up (the viewer shows the request's age); keep `approvals.ttl_s` short where agents retry by id. | [executor](executor.md) |
| Custody covers only the actions routed through the tool executor, the MCP gateway, the LLM gateway and the A2A gateway. | An agent that holds a credential, or can reach an API itself, can act around them. | Keep the credentials only in those services and fence the agents' egress (`agentEgress` in the central chart). | [executor](executor.md#limits), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits), [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits), [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway does not see push notifications an A2A server sends outside it. | The receiver opens that connection to the URL in a message's configuration; the gateway sees only requests sent through it. | Read state through `message/stream` or `tasks/get` (the gateway refuses a message carrying a push notification config) and fence receiver egress. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway relays only `message/send`, `message/stream` and `tasks/get`. | Each method needs its own decision and record. | Cancel or resubscribe directly only if unrecorded access is acceptable, or request support for the method. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#calling-it) |
| **(1.5)** A cut A2A stream has no `tool.result`; its `held_call_unresolved` gap is written only at run end. | The gateway cannot know how the task continued, and the signer finalises open gateway calls at run finalisation. | Read a final run and use `tasks/get` through the gateway for state. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#what-is-recorded) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway reads each Agent Card once, at start. | Re-reading on every message adds a round trip and would let a card change mid-run. | Restart the gateway when a card changes. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** A2A messages name no skill; rules see the skills the card offers. | The protocol has no requested-skill field. | Allow-list `skills` and `route`. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#policy) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway retains at most 1,024 task and context ids per caller and 1,024 callers. | An evicted id cannot be read or continued through the gateway. | Use the task before the bound is exceeded, or retain its state elsewhere. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway checks task ownership only for `tasks/get`'s `id` and messages' `taskId`, `contextId` and `referenceTaskIds`; it forwards free-form `params.metadata` unchanged. | Metadata is outside the gateway's task-reference check. | Do not put a receiver that reads task references from metadata behind the gateway, or deny it with an A2A rule such as `field: target`, `pattern: '"metadata":.*"(taskId|contextId|referenceTaskIds)":'`. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** A transfer is recorded only when code calls `run.transfer` or `tracekit.integrations.c2c.fuse`. | A direct in-process hand-off crosses no capture layer. | Route every transfer through one of those calls. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** Transfer fields are the caller's claims or measurements as marked. | The payload remains in the calling process; the signer cannot check the claimed or measured values. | Pin route and measured weights in policy; retain a copy where appropriate. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#runtransfer) |
| **(1.5)** Transfers are Python-only while experimental; the TypeScript SDK has no `transfer`. | The first integration follows Python C2C research code. | Record transfers with Python `RunHandle.transfer` or `tracekit.integrations.c2c`. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#limits) |
| The agents' egress NetworkPolicy is off by default, and enforced only by a CNI that enforces egress policies. | Turning it on cuts every other connection the agents make; some CNIs ignore egress rules. | Set `agentEgress.enabled` once the agents need nothing else, on a CNI that enforces egress (Calico, Cilium, ...); test it from an agent pod. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |
| The executor and the MCP gateway fail closed: with the signer unreachable nothing runs, whatever the run's fail modes. | They exist to make an action impossible without a record. | Keep the signer available; the agent gets 503 or an `isError` result saying so. | [executor](executor.md#calling-it), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#what-is-recorded) |
| A result the executor or the MCP gateway can't record after the call ran still goes to the agent. | Withholding it would make the agent retry an action that already happened. | They try twice, then say on stderr which call and run; keep the signer reachable. A run that closes with such a call still out waits up to `gateway_wait_s` (default 600 s) past its grace window for the result; then the signer writes a `held_call_unresolved` gap for the call and the verifier warns (`held calls`). | [executor](executor.md#limits) |
| A run with a call the executor or the MCP gateway is still running reaches `run.final` up to `gateway_wait_s` (signer config, default 600 s, the MCP gateway's upstream timeout) after it closes. | The service may still record the call's result, which a final run would refuse. | Lower `gateway_wait_s` to the longest upstream timeout of your gateways if runs must finish sooner. | [executor](executor.md#limits) |
| The events a gateway (the executor, the MCP gateway, the LLM gateway, the OpenShell middleware, the `ext_mcp` processor) writes for an agent count against the gateway's rate for that agent (`limits.events_per_s`, `burst`), its approval waits against its `concurrent_waits` for that agent, and the runs it registers for one (the OpenShell middleware's, one per sandbox) against its `open_runs` for that one; the result of a call it decided counts against nothing. A gateway's total is the per-agent limit times the agents it serves, but for open runs at most `limits.gateway_open_runs` (10,000) of its own and those it registered for all its agents together **(1.4)**; a suspended agent gets no run through a gateway either. | So an agent can't spend the budget its service's records need, nor one agent what the service's other agents need, and a call's result is never refused for rate once its decision was taken. The agent a gateway names is its word, so a cap of its own keeps a gateway naming made-up agents from opening runs without end. | Size `limits` for one agent's traffic through a gateway, and `gateway_open_runs` for the runs one gateway keeps open at once; list only gateways you run under `gateways`. | [executor](executor.md#what-is-recorded) |
| A call the executor or the MCP gateway decided is theirs, for the identity they decided it for: the agent's own `decide`, `approval_request`, `approval_consume`, `approval_abandon` and `complete` on its `tool_call_id`, or the service's for another identity on the run, are refused (`forbidden`), and the executor refuses a `tool_call_id` the agent decided itself. An approval of such a call can't be abandoned; it expires. | Otherwise the agent, holding its run token, could record a forged result or ask for the approval in its own words for a call made with credentials it doesn't hold. | Leave an unanswered approval to expire. The adapters leave the tools the executor helpers build to the executor (the Claude Agent SDK hooks: the tools of the MCP server named `tracekit-executor`); keep a tool you build yourself that calls the executor out of the adapters, or in a Claude Agent SDK server of that name. | [executor](executor.md#what-is-recorded), [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |
| The executor takes only letters, digits and `. _ ~ -` (not all dots) in an `{arg}` of an action's URL path. | A slash, backslash or dot segment, even percent-encoded, can leave its segment once a proxy decodes the path. | Put other values in the query (`?q={q}`, quoted whole) or the JSON body. | [executor](executor.md#configure) |
| The MCP gateway's `trusted` makes each resource of those upstreams count as trusted content, which provenance doesn't hold calls for. Prompts are always recorded untrusted. | A resource anyone can write (an issue, a page, a mail) can carry an injected value; a prompt template echoes the agent's arguments. | List in `trusted` only upstreams whose content neither the agent nor outsiders can write. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| A stdio MCP upstream runs as the MCP gateway's uid, so it can read every other upstream's secret files and the gateway's signer credential. | The gateway starts it as a child process with its own permissions. | Run one gateway (its own identity and secrets) per stdio upstream you don't trust fully; pin each command to an exact version (`npx -y pkg@1.2.3`) or install it in the image. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| A gateway's or executor's call for an agent is authorized as the agent's `scheme:subject` alone: `authorize` entries for an OIDC person or group don't match it, and harness binding doesn't apply to it. | The service names the agent by `caller`, which carries no claims, and authenticates it itself, away from the agent's process. | Grant agents that call through these services by `scheme:subject` in `authorize`; rely on the service's own authenticators, not harness binding, for who may call it. | [executor](executor.md#configure) |
| The MCP gateway lists the first page of each upstream's tools, resources and prompts, reads only resources it listed, and relays no server-initiated messages. | It answers each request with one JSON response and keeps no session. | Keep upstreams' lists to one page; list resources before reading. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| Credentials are cut only as configured: the executor and the MCP gateway replace each secret value of at least 8 characters (of a `<scheme> <credential>` value also the credential, of a Basic one its user:password and password) in the decoded string values they answer and record, in its JSON-escaped, percent-encoded and HTML-escaped forms too, but not a value shorter than that or one an upstream otherwise encodes or transforms (base64, a hash, another case, split across fields). A header value must be one line of printable ASCII. | They match exact strings: a short value ("1", "info") would cut every answer. The line rule keeps a value out of the errors HTTP libraries raise. | Use credentials of 8 characters or more and upstreams that don't echo them; put values that aren't secrets in `settings`, which are never cut. The services refuse to start on a header file that breaks the line rule and name the file. | [executor](executor.md#what-is-recorded), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#what-is-recorded) |
| The MCP gateway neither lists nor calls an upstream tool whose name starts with `prompt:`. | Prompts are decided as `mcp:<upstream>/prompt:<name>`, so such a tool would be decided under a prompt's name. | Rename the tool in the upstream. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| The LiteLLM guardrail decides only the MCP tool calls the LiteLLM proxy runs (`pre_mcp_call`). Tool calls the model returns through LiteLLM's chat, responses or messages endpoints, and MCP listings, resources and prompts, pass undecided. | The agent runs the model's tool calls, so only its side can decide them and record their results; the guardrail sees MCP calls through LiteLLM's hooks. | Gate the agent's own tools with a framework integration and record model exchanges with the [LLM gateway](gateway.md); keep the MCP servers reachable only through LiteLLM. | [LiteLLM](integrations/litellm.md) |
| The LiteLLM guardrail takes the agent's identity from `tracekit_caller` in the metadata of the LiteLLM key: whoever can edit a key's metadata decides which identity its requests act as. LiteLLM passes request headers, `X-Tracekit-Run` included, to its logging callbacks. | LiteLLM authenticates the key, not the agent's Tracekit identity, and treats key metadata as admin configuration (its guardrail opt-outs live there too). | Keep agents' keys away from LiteLLM's key management routes; a run token read from the logs works only for the identity it was issued to. | [LiteLLM](integrations/litellm.md#which-run-which-caller) |
| The LiteLLM guardrail records a result when LiteLLM logs the call: an MCP call answered with an interim input-required result, or one that raised in a `no-log` request, keeps its decision open (`held_call_unresolved` at run end). A `pre_mcp_call` guardrail listed after Tracekit's that rewrites the arguments changes what runs after the decision. | The result comes through LiteLLM's logging hooks, and LiteLLM runs guardrails in the order configured. | List `tracekit` last among `pre_mcp_call` guardrails; read `held_call_unresolved` gaps on runs whose MCP servers ask for input. | [LiteLLM](integrations/litellm.md#what-is-recorded) |
| An `ask` at the LiteLLM guardrail holds the MCP request, and a thread of the proxy's default executor, for up to `approval_wait_s` (default 300 s). An MCP client that times out sooner gets no result, though the call may still run once approved; an approval the guardrail stopped waiting for stays `requested` until it expires, as at the MCP gateway. | The guardrail waits inside LiteLLM's pre-call hook, which LiteLLM awaits before it runs the call. | Set the MCP client's request timeout above `approval_wait_s`, or lower `approval_wait_s`; reject approvals whose caller has given up. | [LiteLLM](integrations/litellm.md#decisions) |
| In the compose stack the custody services' signer identities are uids on the socket the agent container mounts too: an agent container running as root, or keeping the SETUID capability, could connect as one of them. | The signer has one socket and identifies its peers by uid. | Run agent containers as a non-root user with `cap_drop: [ALL]` and `no-new-privileges` (as the example `agent` service does), or deploy on Kubernetes, where each service has its own service-account identity. | [executor](executor.md#compose) |
| The agents' egress NetworkPolicy allows DNS to kube-dns, which a DNS tunnel can use, and doesn't allow a NodeLocal DNSCache (169.254.20.10), so DNS fails where one runs. | Agents need name resolution; the chart can't know the cluster's DNS layout. | Filter DNS (a DNS-aware policy such as Cilium's, or an allow-list in CoreDNS) where tunnelling matters; with NodeLocal DNSCache add an egress rule for `169.254.20.10/32` port 53. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |
| Agents need a projected service-account token per custody service in the central chart, of that service's audience (`<audience>-<name>`), besides the signers'. | A token a custody service receives must not be one the signers take. | Mount one projected token per service the agent calls; see the example. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |
| With an LLM gateway on the same run (or an integration that records the model's messages), each MCP gateway or agentgateway `ext_mcp` processor call, and each executor call made without the model's tool-use id that the signer can't pair with the model's call by arguments, reconciles as `reconcile.fabricated` (plus `reconcile.hook_missing` with the gateway). | They decide under an id of their own: MCP's tools/call carries no tool-use id, and a LangChain or Claude Agent SDK tool handler gets one only from the Tracekit adapter (the LangChain middleware, the Claude Agent SDK's `tracekit_hooks`). | Read such a pair as one call through the gateway or the executor (the decide's source is `gateway`); run the executor helpers under the Tracekit adapter, or pass `tool_call_id` where the framework exposes it, as the OpenAI Agents and Vercel AI helpers do. | [executor](executor.md#limits), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| The MCP gateway holds one session per upstream, shared by every agent and run; one that went away is opened again by the next call, and a call in flight when it went away answers `upstream error` and may have run. | It opens each upstream once and keeps it; it can't tell whether a call cut off mid-flight ran. | Run one gateway per tenant when an upstream keeps per-session state; check an `upstream error` call's effect before making it again. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits) |
| agentgateway's `ext_mcp` processor can't hold a call for a person: an `ask` is refused after 5 s, and passes only when the agent makes the same call again (same run, caller, tool and arguments) after a person approved it. A processor restart forgets pending asks, so the retry asks again. | agentgateway gives a processor 10 s per call. | Tell the agent to retry a call refused with `approval requested` once it is approved; approve with `tracekit approvals`. | [agentgateway](integrations/agentgateway.md#what-happens-to-a-toolscall) |
| agentgateway's `ext_mcp` processor decides only `tools/call`: `resources/read`, `prompts/get` and the other MCP methods go through agentgateway undecided and unrecorded. | The processor speaks for tool calls; a resource or prompt read isn't a call the policy has a class for. | List only `tools/call` in the processor's `methods`, and put MCP servers whose resources or prompts matter behind Tracekit's [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md) instead. | [agentgateway](integrations/agentgateway.md#what-tracekit-records-and-cant-see) |
| A tools/call agentgateway answers with a JSON-RPC error gets no result from the `ext_mcp` processor, and neither does one whose result comes back without `tracekit_call` or after a processor restart: its run ends with a `held_call_unresolved` gap for it. | agentgateway's response hook skips errors and carries no call id of its own. | Keep the `tracekit_call` metadata entry in agentgateway's config (the processor says on stderr when a result names no call it passed); read such a gap as a call that may have failed or run; agentgateway's spans (`mcp.error.code`) show which failed. | [agentgateway](integrations/agentgateway.md#what-tracekit-records-and-cant-see) |
| The `ext_mcp` processor's records are T2 only if the agent reaches its MCP servers only through agentgateway and can't connect to the processor. It listens on loopback without TLS and takes the `caller` and the results it is sent as agentgateway's. | It can't authenticate agentgateway, and it knows the agent only from agentgateway's metadata. | Run it in agentgateway's pod or on its host, never where the agent runs; fence the agent's egress to MCP servers; build `caller` from what agentgateway authenticated (`source.spiffeId`, `jwt.sub`), never from a header. | [agentgateway](integrations/agentgateway.md#what-tracekit-records-and-cant-see) |
| agentgateway forwards the agent's headers, `X-Tracekit-Run` included, to HTTP MCP servers on every request but a `tools/call` the `ext_mcp` processor passes, whose answer removes it. | It passes client headers through to its targets, and only `tools/call` reaches the processor. | None needed: a run token is bound to the agent's identity and is no use without the agent's credentials. | [agentgateway](integrations/agentgateway.md#the-ext_mcp-processor) |
| A Wassette permission grant approved through the MCP gateway stays in effect after the call: every later call of that component, from every agent and run behind the gateway, has it, after restarts too, until it is revoked. | Wassette saves permissions with the component, not per run or caller; the approval covers the grant call. | Serve a fixed tool set (`--manifest`, `--disable-builtin-tools`); otherwise run one Wassette per tenant and revoke grants (`revoke-*-permission`, `reset-permission`) when the work is done. | [Wassette](integrations/wassette.md#what-tracekit-records-and-doesnt-see) |
| Tracekit doesn't see what a Wassette component does inside Wassette, or permissions set outside the MCP gateway (the manifest, the `wassette permission` command, a component's policy file, an MCP client connected to Wassette directly). The manifest isn't signed into the run. | Only calls through the gateway reach the signer. | Let only the gateway reach Wassette (on loopback, on the gateway's host), and keep its manifest and component directory writable only by whoever deploys it. | [Wassette](integrations/wassette.md#what-tracekit-records-and-doesnt-see) |
| A Wassette component's own failure (a host its permissions don't allow: `{"err":"ErrorCode::HttpRequestDenied"}`) is recorded as an `ok` result. | Wassette returns it in a normal result, `isError` false, and the gateway records the status the upstream gives. | Read the result, not only its status. | [Wassette](integrations/wassette.md#what-tracekit-records-and-doesnt-see) |
| The TypeScript executor helpers cover OpenAI Agents and the Vercel AI SDK, not the Claude Agent SDK. | Its `tool()` takes only a zod shape, and actions declare JSON Schema; converting needs a dependency. | Write a `tool()` per action with its zod shape whose handler returns `action.call(args)`. | [executor](executor.md#calling-it) |
| OpenAI Agents' shell and apply_patch tools don't run the signer's copy of T2 arguments; function tools do. | They run the SDK's action object, which the gate read just before it runs. | Route such actions through the executor if they need T2. | [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |
