| The Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Qwen Code, Factory Droid and Antigravity CLI hooks can't replace a call's input: under T2 they run the arguments the signer consumed, which it checked against the approved ones. | Those integrations take no updated input from Tracekit's hook; Qwen Code, Factory Droid and Antigravity CLI (`overwrite`) would, but Tracekit sends it only to Claude Code. | Use Claude Code or the executor where the approved copy itself must run. | [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |
| OpenCode passes one `output.args` through every plugin's `tool.execute.before` in turn, so a plugin that runs after Tracekit's can change a call's arguments after Tracekit decided it; the record shows the arguments Tracekit decided. | The plugin API lets each plugin edit the same arguments, and Tracekit can't see what a later plugin does. | Keep Tracekit's the only plugin that edits tool arguments, or use the [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md) or the [executor](executor.md) where the decided copy must be the one that runs. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| The OpenShell middleware decides only the HTTP requests OpenShell allows to the hosts its policy entry includes, and their responses: not other hosts, not traffic OpenShell can't inspect as HTTP, and not WebSocket messages; without its OCSF import, OpenShell's own network-policy decisions aren't in the run either. A WebSocket upgrade is decided, but its call never completes, so the run ends with a `held_call_unresolved` gap for it. | OpenShell selects middleware by destination host and passes it only the HTTP it inspects; the middleware doesn't take WebSocket messages, and OpenShell runs no response middleware on an upgrade. | List every host the sandbox's network policy allows under the entry's `endpoints.include`, keep `on_error: fail_closed`, and turn on the OCSF import (`ocsf`) to record OpenShell's own blocks. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| A request the policy asks about is denied at once (`tracekit_approval_required`); it runs only when the agent sends the same request again after a person approves it. A retry that changes a header (a trace or idempotency id) is a new request and asks again. | OpenShell waits for a middleware's answer at most 30 s, and the approval is bound to the exact request. | Approve with `tracekit approvals approve`, then let the agent retry; where asks matter, use clients that send the same headers on a retry. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#held-requests) |
| The OpenShell middleware denies a request whose body isn't UTF-8 text (gzip, protobuf, a binary upload) or is over 512 KiB (`tracekit_body_unreadable`), whatever the policy, and the run has no record of it (the middleware prints it on stderr). A text body over 64 KiB is denied by every rule that reads the whole call (`TK-OVERSIZE`, the server pack's secret rule among them). | The policy decides on what it can read; a decision on a body's digest would let a compressed or padded secret through the content rules. | Send request bodies as uncompressed text under 64 KiB to the hosts the middleware's entry includes; route a host that needs binary uploads through another policy entry, where OpenShell's own policy governs it. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-the-agent-sees) |
| A response body OpenShell doesn't offer to middleware (compressed, partial, bodyless, `no-transform`), a binary one or one over 512 KiB completes its call with a digest. Under a policy that lists `http_request` as untrusted (the server pack), that makes the run's provenance index `partial`, and `from: untrusted` rules then hold every request that sends data. | The signer can index only content it receives. | Have the agent's HTTP client ask for uncompressed responses (`Accept-Encoding: identity`) where provenance rules matter. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-records), [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| Without `gateway_jwt` the OpenShell middleware takes any peer that reaches its address for OpenShell, so it refuses to listen beyond loopback unless `allow_unauthenticated: true`. With it, an OpenShell token is a bearer credential until it expires (an hour at most), and one gateway's key signs every sandbox's. | OpenShell authenticates extension calls with a short-lived EdDSA token only, and sends none over `allow_insecure_transport`. | Set `gateway_jwt` (the gateway's `gateway_jwt.gateway_id`, its public key, the registration's audience) with `tls`; never include the middleware's address in a sandbox's network policy. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#who-may-call-it) |
| At most 64 responses stream through the OpenShell middleware at once, shared by every sandbox: one sandbox's slow downloads can hold all 64 (OpenShell's deadline is per exchange, not per stream). One more is refused (`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`), which OpenShell handles by the entry's `on_error` (`fail_closed`: the sandbox doesn't get that response), and its call ends as a `held_call_unresolved` gap. | Each open response stream holds a thread; the threads kept for request evaluations must stay free, or every sandbox's requests would wait. | Keep hosts the sandboxes download large or slow responses from out of the middleware's `endpoints` (another policy entry); the middleware prints each refused response on stderr. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| The OpenShell middleware speaks supervisor-middleware protocol 1.0 only. It refuses OpenShell before 0.1.0 (NemoClaw pins 0.0.116) and any other protocol version, and the OpenShell gateway then doesn't start. | OpenShell's middleware API is still changing; a version the middleware doesn't know may change what a message means. | Run OpenShell 0.1.0 or newer; upgrade Tracekit when OpenShell changes the protocol version. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#requirements) |
| The OpenShell middleware keeps each sandbox's run, its held approvals and its open requests in memory. After a restart a sandbox gets a new run, a request approved before it asks again, and the response to a request allowed before it isn't recorded (that call ends as a `held_call_unresolved` gap). Replicas behind one endpoint each register their own run per sandbox, and with `ocsf` replicas under one signer identity stop each other's OCSF import (the signer refuses the process another took over from, `conflict`). | The run tokens never leave the process. | Restart it rarely and run one replica per endpoint; read a sandbox's runs together (their ids start `openshell-<sandbox id>-`). | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-records) |
| The OCSF import's calls count against the middleware's own event rate (`limits.events_per_s`, `burst`: 200 a second by default), one budget for every sandbox's events, not one per sandbox as its calls have: around 100 busy sandboxes spend it, and then OpenShell's batches are refused (`UNAVAILABLE`) and sent again, and those it gives up on become its gap records. | The import is one observer, the middleware's identity; its effects are attributed to the sandboxes' runs only by their run tokens. | Raise the middleware identity's `limits` in `signer.yaml` with the number of sandboxes; read a run's `observer_lost` gaps for what was dropped. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |
| OpenShell's OCSF events are recorded as OpenShell reports them: a supervisor or gateway that reports falsely is believed. One bearer token authenticates the gateway and every supervisor, so an event's sandbox is the one the sending OpenShell component names. | OpenShell's OCSF export carries only the collector headers its gateway is configured with, the same for every supervisor, and its events are unsigned. | Set `ocsf.token_file` with `tls`; read OpenShell's events as its report, beside the requests Tracekit decided itself. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#who-may-call-it) |
| The OCSF import records OpenShell's network, process and policy-change events only. Other classes (SSH, authentication, detection findings other than a `fail_open` bypass, which is a signed gap, application lifecycle, base events), network events that neither allow nor deny, and events naming no sandbox are counted on the middleware's stderr, not recorded. OpenShell reports its sandbox's entrypoint, not each process the agent starts. | Tracekit's records have no form for those classes yet; OpenShell's process events cover only what it starts. | Keep OpenShell's own OCSF JSONL output (`[openshell.gateway.ocsf_log]`, `ocsf_json_enabled`) for the rest; run a [kernel sensor](integrations/tetragon.md) to see every process. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| OpenShell's OCSF export is best-effort: it drops events when its queue is full or a batch fails four times (its next batch reports how many, which becomes a signed gap), and events lost while it shuts down get no report. A sandbox whose export stops is noticed only once the middleware has decided one of its requests, 60 s later. | OpenShell counts its own drops but numbers no events, so a loss it doesn't report can only be inferred from silence; every request the middleware decides is one event OpenShell should send. | Keep the middleware and the signer reachable from every supervisor; read a run's `observer_lost` gaps before trusting the absence of a block. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |
| The OCSF import covers only the runs the middleware registered (the sandboxes'): when the middleware stops or restarts, each of those still open gets an `observer_lost` gap; other runs of the tenant get none. An event for a sandbox whose run the signer already closed (`idle_s`) is an `effect_outside_run` gap in the tenant's run `tracekit/signer`, until the sandbox's next request opens a new run. | The import names the middleware's identity as its one caller (`callers`), so it covers that identity's runs alone; the middleware learns that a run closed only from its next call for that sandbox. | Set `idle_s` above the sandboxes' quiet periods. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |
| Behind the MCP gateway, Docker's MCP Gateway with its dynamic tools on (`mcp-find`, `mcp-add`, `mcp-exec`, `code-mode` and others) adds servers and runs other tools from inside one call, which Tracekit decides as that one call. | Docker's gateway makes those calls itself; the MCP gateway decides only what it forwards. | Start each Docker upstream with `--servers=<server>`, which turns them off (or `docker mcp feature disable dynamic-tools`). | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| Every server behind one `docker mcp gateway run` upstream is decided under that upstream's name (`mcp:<upstream>/<tool>`), so rules keyed on the server (`mcp:fetch/*`) don't tell them apart. | The MCP gateway names a call by its upstream, and Docker's gateway doesn't prefix its servers' tools by default. | Give each Docker server its own upstream, named after the server. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| The MCP gateway doesn't cut the secrets Docker's MCP Gateway gives its servers from what it answers and records, and doesn't see the image, mounts or network a call ran with. | It knows only the `env` and `headers` values in its own config; Docker keeps its secrets and containers to itself. | Keep Docker's `--block-secrets` on (the default): it refuses a call whose arguments or text result look like they carry a secret. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| An agent that can use Docker (the `docker` group, a mounted Docker socket, Docker Desktop as its own user) can start Docker's MCP Gateway itself and call its servers around Tracekit. | Custody covers only the calls routed through the MCP gateway. | Run the agent as a user without Docker access, and the MCP gateway as one with it. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| Docker's MCP Gateway's OpenTelemetry spans (`mcp.tool.call`) aren't imported as tool calls: the OTLP import counts and skips them. | They carry Docker's own attributes (`mcp.tool.name`, `mcp.server.name`), not the conventions the import reads, and no arguments or results. | Record Docker's tool calls through the MCP gateway. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |

## Egress observation (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| The egress observer sees hosts, ports, byte counts and times, never paths, headers or content: a call to an explained host can still carry anything. | It doesn't intercept TLS, so it never holds the agents' traffic in clear or a CA they would have to trust. | Route actions whose content matters through the executor or the MCP gateway; read `effect.observed` as where the run went, not what it sent. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-recorded) |
| Attribution by identity (no run token in the proxy URL) gives an observation to the identity's latest open run, which is ambiguous when one identity runs several at once. The record says `attribution: identity`. | The proxy knows who connected, not for which run, unless the client says so. | Set `HTTPS_PROXY=https://<run_id>:<run token>@...` per run (`attribution: token`); in system mode one agent user runs one session at a time. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| `posture` (`network: enforced`, `tool_calls: enforced`) and `gateway_mandatory` are what the operator asserts in signer.yaml: `run.registered` records them with `attested: false`, and the matrix's `enforced` for model calls, tool calls and network rests on them (`asserted`). Without `tool_calls: enforced`, tool calls are at most `reported`, even when every decision came through the executor or the MCP gateway. | The signer can't see the cluster's network policy, where provider keys live, or whether the agent has another way to act than the calls it saw. | Back it with the central chart's `agentEgress` NetworkPolicy on a CNI that enforces egress, keys only in the LLM gateway, agents with no tools of their own, and test each from an agent pod. | [egress](egress.md#posture) |
| A host only `explains` covers (CDNs, redirects, package indexes) is `reconcile.unexplained_effect` until it is listed, so a fresh deployment sees many. The server pack only notifies on them (shadow). | The signer knows the hosts of http calls, not what a shell command or a library contacts beneath them. | Tune the policy's `explains` from the warnings (the detail names the host) before you set `unexplained_effect` to enforce. | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| An `explains` host glob over a public suffix of two labels (`*.co.uk`, `*.github.io`) passes lint and explains every host under it, whoever holds it. | Lint only requires a dot and two or more labels after the last wildcard; it has no public suffix list, and `explains` is the operator's own policy. | Write the narrowest host you mean: the hosts themselves, or a suffix one owner holds (`*.cdn.example.co.uk`). | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| A laptop without Linux system mode's `--egress` has no observer: its network is `reported` (http calls) or `not covered`. `--egress` needs Linux with iptables' owner match; macOS has none. | Only a rule outside the agent's user can force its traffic through the observer. | Use [system mode](deploy.md#laptop-system-mode)'s init with `--v2 --egress` on Linux, or run agents on a server deployment behind the egress observer. | [egress](egress.md#linux-system-mode) |
| An observer covers the runs of its tenant registered while it is alive, and which runs it covers lives in the signer's memory: a run registered before its first heartbeat, or open across a signer restart, is not `observed`. After a restart with no snapshot since, a run's earlier http calls no longer explain a host. | The heartbeat watch is the transcript tailer's, which keeps no state in the log. | Start the observer before the agents; re-check runs that span a signer restart. | [egress](egress.md#heartbeats-and-coverage) |
| A run an observer covers reaches `run.final` up to `observe_grace_s` (default 120 s) after it closes; an observation after that goes into the tenant's signer run as `effect_outside_run`, not reconciled. | The observer reports each minute once it ends, at its next heartbeat; a final run takes nothing more. | Raise `observe_grace_s` if `effect_outside_run` gaps name late runs; export them with `--run-set`. | [egress](egress.md#late-observations) |
| One attribution (a connection's identity, else its run) has at most 64 tunnels open through the egress proxy at once; one more is closed before it reaches its host. On Linux a loopback client's uid has at most 64 connections open, counted from accept, handshakes included, and every client sends what attributes it within 5 s. The proxy takes at most 256 connections per client address: in Linux system mode every agent connects from loopback, and each uid keeps the others' share; elsewhere (no uid to read, or one address for many sandboxes) one client's half-open connections can still take that address's 256, each for 5 s. | So one agent can't hold every connection its address may open, which would lock the other agents out. | Reuse connections (HTTP keep-alive, HTTP/2) in agents that open many at once; run agents that need more under identities of their own; give each untrusted client an address of its own. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| The proxy reads the server name from the ClientHello's first TLS record only, knows the public DNS-over-HTTPS endpoints by name only, and lets an `allow`ed host through with no SNI check (any protocol). | A split ClientHello is rare; DoH on another host is HTTPS to that host, which is recorded as such. | Keep `allow` to the hosts that need it; DoH elsewhere shows as traffic to its host. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| Only connections made with a run's `proxy_env` (`proxyEnv`) carry its token: the SDKs never set the agent process's own `HTTPS_PROXY`, so its in-process connections reach the observer only when the deployment proxies them, attributed by identity (see the next row). | Proxy settings are per process and its clients read them once; the run token is per run, and one process can hold several runs. | Give `run.proxy_env` to the commands each run starts and to the HTTP clients it uses; set the agent's `HTTPS_PROXY` in the deployment for the rest, with the signers and custody services in `NO_PROXY`. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| The executor and the gateways reach their upstreams directly, not through the observer: their calls are recorded at T2 already, and the agents' NetworkPolicy doesn't restrict them. | Their records name each call's host; an MCP gateway's upstream sessions are shared by every run, so no one run token fits them. | Read their records for where they went; restrict their own egress with the cluster's network policy. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| A host the observer's `allow` doesn't list must resolve to public addresses only: a name resolving to a loopback, private or link-local address is refused (403), internal services included. | Otherwise the proxy would open the agents' way to everything only it can reach (cluster services, cloud metadata). | Add internal hosts agents may reach to `allow`, or to the agent's `NO_PROXY` when it reaches them directly; the 403 names the address. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| An observer covers only the runs of its own tenant (signer.yaml `tenants`). | Its observations are written as its tenant's records. | Run one observer per tenant. | [egress](egress.md#configure) |
| An observer that heartbeats after a run closes and then dies before its next report leaves the run `network: observed` with no `observer_lost` gap: the totals of its last minute, before the close, are lost. | Coverage rests on heartbeats; a beat inside `observe_grace_s` counts as covering the run, and nothing marks that the last minute's report followed. | Keep `observe_grace_s` at 90 s or more (two report intervals), run the observer under a supervisor that restarts it, and read a short-lived run's `observed` network with this in mind. | [egress](egress.md) |
| Attempts the proxy refuses (a raw IP, a private or metadata address, a DNS-over-HTTPS endpoint, an SNI mismatch, a name that doesn't resolve) leave no signed record; only the agent sees the refusal. The proxy resolves every name an agent sends it, so a name can carry data out through DNS (`<data>.evil.example`) whether or not the connection is then refused. | The observer records the connections it opens; a refusal comes before any, and resolving the name is how it decides. | Treat the observer as a record of connections, not of attempts; to catch exfiltration through DNS, log or restrict the resolver the proxy uses. | [egress](egress.md) |
| The observer keeps at most 10,000 observation totals the signer refused, and sends them again with every heartbeat; past that it drops the oldest, says how many on stderr, and they leave no signed record. | A refusal that never changes would otherwise grow the queue without end. | Fix what the observer's stderr names (its grants, its tenant) before the queue fills, and watch that stderr for `dropped`. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| Model calls explain only the public API host of their provider (`api.openai.com`, `api.anthropic.com`). | The record names the provider, not the endpoint. | Send other endpoints through the LLM gateway, which makes those connections itself. | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| Over the network (the central chart, compose) a plain `HTTPS_PROXY` client without a run token is refused (407): attribution by identity there needs `Proxy-Authorization: Bearer <token>` or a client certificate, which proxy settings can't carry. Only system mode attributes by identity alone (the agent's uid). | The proxy knows a networked client only by what it sends. | Give the agent process a run's `proxy_env` URL as its proxy, or a client that sends a bearer token of the egress audience. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| The proxy checks each run token with the signer before it opens a connection: one round trip per connection, which costs none of the observer's event rate, and while the signer can't be asked, requests with a run token are refused (503, saying so). | Only the signer can check a token (an HMAC with its secret); an unchecked one would let a client name any run. | Keep the signer reachable from the observer; clients retry a 503. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| Totals the signer can't take, or refuses, wait in the proxy's memory (a refused one is sent again with every heartbeat); a killed proxy loses what it had not reported, up to a minute and a heartbeat's. Any restart of the proxy, even after SIGTERM sent everything (a rolling update too), is an `observer_lost` gap in each open run it covered, and so a second proxy process under the same identity: run one replica per observer identity. | It reports per minute to keep records few; the signer can't tell a clean restart from a crash, nor a second process from a restart. | Stop it with SIGTERM, which sends every total, and restart it while no run is open; read its stderr line on a refusal. | [egress](egress.md#heartbeats-and-coverage) |
| Linux system mode's firewall rule lets the agent's uid reach every loopback address, so a loopback service can send on for it unobserved: a local DNS stub (systemd-resolved's `127.0.0.53`) resolves any name the agent asks, and the lookups themselves can carry data. | The rule allows loopback for the observer and the agent's own local services; it can't tell the DNS stub from them. | Reject the agent uid's DNS to loopback (port 53) in your firewall if all its clients use the proxy, which resolves names itself; otherwise read the resolver's query log. | [egress](egress.md#linux-system-mode) |
| Plain HTTP requests through the proxy need a `Content-Length` (no chunked request bodies); HTTPS goes through CONNECT and is unaffected. | The proxy forwards one request per connection. | Use HTTPS, or send a length; the proxy answers 411 saying so. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| Through microsandbox the observer sees hosts (each connection's TLS server name), ports, bytes and times: never paths, headers or content, nothing microsandbox resolves through its DNS forwarder, nothing sent to `host.microsandbox.internal`, and nothing inside the microVM. | It doesn't intercept TLS; microsandbox sends DNS and host-local connections around the proxy. | Restrict DNS and host-local access with microsandbox's network policy; route actions whose content matters through the executor or the MCP gateway. | [microsandbox](integrations/microsandbox.md#what-the-observer-doesnt-see) |
| Over SOCKS5 (microsandbox) a connection to an address reaches it only when its TLS server name resolves to that address on the observer's host: plain HTTP and other non-TLS TCP are closed unless `allow` lists the address, and UDP (SOCKS5 UDP ASSOCIATE) is refused. | microsandbox sends the address it resolved, never a name; the server name is the only name the observer sees, and it records one only when its own lookup agrees. | Use HTTPS; list fixed addresses the sandboxes need in `allow`; QUIC clients fall back to TCP. The stderr line names each closed connection. | [microsandbox](integrations/microsandbox.md#what-the-run-gets) |
| A name whose DNS answers change between microsandbox's lookup and the observer's (a CDN rotating addresses) can close a connection. | The observer relays to the address microsandbox chose, which its policy checked, and records the name only when its own answer includes that address. | Point microsandbox's `nameservers` at the resolver the observer's host uses, ideally a caching one; retry a closed connection. | [microsandbox](integrations/microsandbox.md#what-the-run-gets) |
| Per-run attribution from microsandbox needs per-sandbox SOCKS5 credentials (the run id and token); a proxy enforced in microsandbox's managed configuration replaces them, which leaves attribution by identity: the uid running `msb` on Linux (its latest open run), and on macOS no attribution, so every connection is refused. On Linux that uid goes with the token too, so a run registered by another identity is attributed by the uid. | microsandbox's managed proxy is one object for every sandbox on the host, credentials included; the observer reads a loopback client's uid only on Linux. | Set credentials per sandbox from the code that creates it, and register the run as the user that runs `msb`; enforce the proxy in managed configuration only where one user runs one run at a time. | [microsandbox](integrations/microsandbox.md#setup) |
| A SOCKS5 password holds at most 255 bytes, and a run token grows with the name of the identity that registered the run: a long one doesn't fit. | RFC 1929 gives the password one length byte; the token carries the run id, tenant and identity. | Register runs under a short identity (a uid, a short SPIFFE ID). | [microsandbox](integrations/microsandbox.md#setup) |
| E2B's `egressProxy` works only on E2B Cloud and BYOC, for teams that have it enabled; deployments built from the open-source `e2b-dev/runtime` reject it. Its client isn't open source: Tracekit is tested against E2B's API description, not E2B itself. Without it, a proxy set in the sandbox's environment (`envs`) is the agent's to ignore. | E2B gates the feature per team, and only its closed builds carry the proxy client. | Ask E2B to enable `egressProxy` for your team; check that a sandbox's first connections show up as `effect.observed` before you rely on it. | [E2B](integrations/e2b.md) |
| E2B tunnels TCP only: a sandbox's UDP (DNS, QUIC/HTTP3) leaves directly, unobserved. Plain HTTP and other non-TLS TCP through E2B reach only hosts and addresses `allow` lists. | E2B's egress proxy is SOCKS5 CONNECT; the observer names a host only by a TLS server name it can check. | Drop the UDP a sandbox doesn't need with `denyOut` (E2B applies it to non-TCP traffic in this mode) and keep `allowOut` to the hosts it needs; use HTTPS. | [E2B](integrations/e2b.md#what-the-observer-doesnt-see) |
| E2B's SDKs send no `tls` for `egressProxy`, and off loopback the observer takes SOCKS5 only inside TLS: a sandbox created through the SDKs with the observer as its proxy has no network. | The run token is the SOCKS5 password, and the observer takes no credentials in clear off loopback. | Create the sandbox through E2B's API with `network.egressProxy.tls.enabled: true`. | [E2B](integrations/e2b.md#setup) |
| The run token E2B holds as the SOCKS5 password lets anyone holding it send traffic through the observer as that run. | The observer attributes a connection by the token it presents. A token alone can't write to the run: the signer checks it against the identity it was issued to. | Close each run when its sandbox ends: an observation for a final run goes to the tenant's signer run as `effect_outside_run`. | [E2B](integrations/e2b.md#setup) |
| The connections of every sandbox on one microsandbox host (loopback) or one E2B node come from one address, which shares the observer's per-address limits (256 open connections, failed run token checks); on Linux every sandbox of one `msb` user also shares that uid's 64 open connections. | The limits stop one client from taking every slot; the observer knows a client by its address. | Keep sandboxes' long-lived connections few; spread E2B sandboxes across several observer addresses. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |

## Followed sandbox logs (`tracekit follow`)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A followed record is agentsh's word, signed when it arrived (`policy.external`, source `import`, tier T3). Tracekit doesn't check that agentsh logged everything, or agentsh's own hash chain. | The follower reads the log agentsh writes; only agentsh sees its decisions. | Check agentsh's log with its own tools as well (`agentsh audit verify`); each gap's reason says where to look. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| The follower's identity is trusted to import agentsh's decisions: an agent that can present it can import made-up ones. | `importers` and `decision_import` are grants to an identity; the signer can't check agentsh's log. | Give the follower an identity of its own, such as a signer token readable only by the user the follower runs as. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#setup) |
| The signer watches a follower's runs for heartbeats from memory: a run open across a signer restart with no snapshot since isn't watched, so a follower killed then leaves no gap. | The watch is the transcript tailer's, which keeps no state in the log. | Restart the signer while the follower's run is closed (after the signer's `idle_s` without a decision), or read a run that spans a restart with this in mind. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#what-tracekit-records) |
| Without agentsh's `audit.integrity`, its lines carry no sequence, so a line missing from the log is no gap. | The sequence comes from agentsh's integrity chain. | Turn on `audit.integrity` in agentsh's config. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#setup) |
| `tracekit follow agentsh` starts at the log's end: what agentsh wrote before, or while the follower was stopped, isn't imported. | The follower keeps no position on disk. | Start the follower before agentsh's sessions; a stop is a gap that says where it stopped. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#how-it-works) |
| One run holds all of agentsh's log, and a record doesn't name agentsh's session: that is in the committed line only. | The follower follows one file, which every session writes to. | Find the line in agentsh's log by the record's `tool_use_id`, agentsh's event `id`. | [agentsh](integrations/agentsh.md#what-tracekit-records) |

## Kernel sensor import

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tracekit records only what the sensor exported and the import read: events no TracingPolicy or Falco rule selected, events an export filter or field filter dropped, alerts whose output lacks a field the import needs, and events before the import's first heartbeat aren't in the run, and nothing marks them. Connects to no IP address (unix sockets) are skipped, counted on the import's stderr only. | The import sees the sensor's output, nothing else; the kernel side is the sensor's. | Use the sample TracingPolicy and export allowlist, or the sample Falco rules; check once that a test agent's exec, connect and write outside the workspace each show up as `effect.observed`. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt), [Falco](integrations/falco.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| Tetragon numbers no events: only the drops it reports in its export (`rate_limit_info`, `process_throttle`) become signed gaps. Drops it counts only in its metrics (ring buffer, event queue, export failures) don't. | A missing event can't be seen in a stream without sequence numbers. | Alert on `tetragon_observer_ringbuf_events_lost_total`, `tetragon_observer_ringbuf_queue_events_lost_total` and `tetragon_events_export_failed_total`; leave `--export-rate-limit` off or high. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#losses) |
| A loss is a gap in every run the import covers (the open runs of its `runs` entries' callers), whether or not the dropped events were those runs'; a run registered before the import's first heartbeat gets none (a loss while it covers no run goes to the tenant's signer run). | The sensors' loss reports name no process; coverage is the runs the import covered throughout. | Start the import before the agents; read `observer_lost` gaps next to the sensor's metrics. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#losses) |
| One import identity is one process: a second process of it calling is taken for a restart (an `observer_lost` gap in each run it covers), and the first is refused from then on (`conflict`). With an import per node, each with its own identity, an import's restart, silence or loss is a gap in the open runs of every node, and a run says its processes were observed only by the import that recorded at least one of its events, so a run with none says not observed. | The signer knows a run's identity, not the node its processes run on. | Give each node's import its own identity; or run one import for the cluster that reads every node's export (gathered by your log pipeline), which covers each run alone and gaps only for its own losses. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#setup) |
| The records are the import's assertion of what the sensor said. Tracekit doesn't check them, and a root user on the node can change the sensor, its output or the import. | The signer can check who sent an event, not whether the kernel saw it. | Give the import its own identity, never an agent's (the signer refuses an import that owns the run); keep agents unprivileged on the node. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| **(1.4)** An observer's identity is of one kind while the signer runs (one sensor's import, or an egress observer): a call as another kind is refused. A run whose owner the import's `runs` entries stop naming is no longer covered by it, so its `run.final` says processes not observed. | `run.final` says what covered a run by the kind of each observer covering it. | Give each import and each egress proxy its own identity; restart the signer to give one a new kind. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#attribution) |
| One import maps processes to at most 256 identities (its `runs` entries' callers). | Every call names them, so the signer covers only their runs. | Run one import per group of agent identities. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#attribution) |
| An event goes to the latest open run of the identity its `runs` entry names registered by the event's time, which is ambiguous when one identity runs several at once; the time is the sensor's clock, so with it behind the signer's an event can go to the run before (one still open) or, more than 5 s behind with none open, be an `effect_outside_run` gap. | A sensor's events name the pod, container and uid, not the run. | One identity per concurrently running agent, or one pod per run; keep the nodes and the signer on NTP. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#attribution) |
| An event is joined to a tool call only through `CODEX_TOOL_CALL_ID`, which the agent's process can change or remove; Claude Code exports no such id. An effect nothing explains isn't reconciled: no `reconcile.unexplained_effect`, no count in `run.final`'s coverage. | The id is the only link between a process and a call; a process with none can't be told from one that hid it. | Run `tracekit analyze --v2` on the finished run: TK-X206 joins each effect to a call by its claim, its parent process or the call's time, and flags what none explains. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#checking-effects-against-calls) |
| The binary, file paths and connected addresses are stored in the clear; the arguments are committed to, not stored. | Auditors need what ran and where; arguments can hold secrets. | Test arguments from Tetragon's own export against the commitment with the record's revealed salt (`tracekit signer reveal`). | [format v2](format-v2.md#2-hashes-over-agent-content) |
| With `--follow` the import reads from the file's end on: events written while it was down aren't imported (its restart is an `observer_lost` gap). It doesn't read rotated or compressed files. | Reading the file again from the start would record events twice. | Run the import as a service that restarts, next to Tetragon. | [Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md#setup) |
| Falco reports a second's drops only when more than `syscall_event_drops.threshold` (10 %) of its events were dropped, at most one second per 1/`rate` (30 s) by default, and needs `priority: debug` for the report at all; alerts dropped from its full output queue show only in its log and metrics. | Those are Falco's settings for its drop alert; the queue has no alert. | Use the Falco settings in the setup (`threshold: 0`, `rate: 1`); alert on `falcosecurity_falco_outputs_queue_num_drops_total`. | [Falco](integrations/falco.md#losses) |
| A Falco restart leaves no mark in its alerts, so what happened while it was down is missing with no gap. A Tetragon restart is a gap: it reports the processes already running (`procFS`). | Falco writes no alert when it starts. | Alert on Falco's restarts in your monitoring (its pod's restart count); prefer Tetragon where a restart must be in the run. | [Falco](integrations/falco.md#losses) |
| With Falco, only alerts of rules tagged `tracekit` are recorded, and none of a process's exits; records have no `exec_id`. | Other rules' alerts would record the same system call twice; the sample rules match no exit, and Falco names no process across executions. | Use the sample rules; join a process's records by `pid` and time. | [Falco](integrations/falco.md#what-is-recorded) |
| A bundle with kernel sensor records (effects, or an `observer_lost` gap of its import) needs verifier 1.4.0 or later. | Older verifiers don't know the records' fields. | Verify with 1.4.0 or later: [verdicts](verdicts.md#a-bundle-that-needs-a-newer-verifier) has the commands. | [format v2](format-v2.md#3-events) |

## Signed findings

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A signed finding reads `independent` only when your trust config pins its author in `analyzers`; otherwise `unpinned author <id>`, and `self-asserted` when the author is the analysed run's identity or a run's signer runs as its client's user. Every finding of the dev signer is `self-asserted`. | The signer measures who wrote a finding; only you can say whom you trust to analyse runs. | Run `tracekit analyze --v2` as an operator identity, never the agent's (`tracekit doctor` warns: `D-ANALYZER-AGENT`), and pin it in your trust config: `"analyzers": ["uid:1004"]`. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#reviews) |
| A signed finding is the analyzer's word. The verifiers check that its citations are records of the analysed run with those hashes, not that the check was right, ran on every record, or found everything; a missing finding proves nothing. | A finding holds no text of the run, so a verifier can't run the checks again from a bundle. | Read the cited records (`tracekit view`), or run `tracekit analyze --v2` again with access to the content vault. | [format v2](format-v2.md#3-events) |
| A bundle of a findings run without the run it analyses verifies with its findings not counted (`reviews counted` warning) and their author not checked. | The citations and the author's comparison need the analysed run's records. | Export the analysed run with `tracekit export --v2 --run R --with-findings`. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#reviews) |
| A findings run or a `tracekit why` replay run can't itself be analysed or replayed: the signer refuses to register a findings or replay run about one (`invalid_request`), so `tracekit analyze --v2 --run` on one prints its findings unsigned and exits 2. | Findings and replays are about the agent's run they name; findings about findings say nothing of what the agent did. | Analyse or replay the run it names (`analyzes` or `replay_of` in its `run.registered`). | [findings](findings.md), [why](why.md) |
| The verdict of a signed finding is a closed schema: a check whose finding needs another rule, field or reason code needs a new RPC version, in the signer and the analyzer. | The schema is how the signer keeps text of the run (excerpts, paths, reasons) out of records that can't be erased. | Upgrade the signer with the analyzer. | [format v2](format-v2.md#3-events) |
| A bundle with signed findings needs verifier 1.4.0 or later. A v1 judge verdict carried into a v2 run (a `review` without citations) is counted as `imported`, never checked. | Older verifiers don't know `cites`; an imported verdict cites nothing to check. | Verify with 1.4.0 or later: [verdicts](verdicts.md#a-bundle-that-needs-a-newer-verifier) has the commands. | [format v2](format-v2.md#3-events) |

## Witnesses and monitor

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A witness you run yourself (`operator` class) gives only `Assurance: local`. | Whoever runs the stack runs the witness too. | Have someone else run a witness (`customer`, `public`) on infrastructure you can't change. | [deploy compose](deploy-compose.md#what-witnessed-needs) |
| Witness classes come from the verifier's judgement, never from the bundle. | The bundle's producer can't vouch for its own witnesses. | Get each witness key from the witness's operator. | [auditor guide](auditor-guide.md#2-build-the-trust-config-from-independent-sources) |
| A git witness without a remote, or a file witness on the same host, adds little. | Whoever controls the host can rewrite it. | Use a remote with a protected branch, or storage the host can't rewrite. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#trust-model-per-witness-type) |
| An unreachable witness delays cosigning; the gap is signed, not prevented. | The signer can't force a witness to answer. | Watch `witness_failed` gaps and `tracekit_signer_witness_lag_records`. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#v2-c2sp-tlog-witnesses) |
| The v1 Rekor witness is experimental, off by default and not run against the live service. | It is tested offline only. | Treat its first real run as validation. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#rekor-experimental) |
| Rekor entries are public and permanent and reveal activity timing; the publishing key links a signer's anchors. | Rekor is a public transparency log. | Use Rekor only where that is acceptable. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#v2-rekor-v2-anchors-and-rfc-3161-timestamps) |
| Rekor v2 anchoring needs a signing config that lists a Rekor v2 log; the TUF files are pinned local copies, not fetched. | Production Sigstore may not list one yet; TUF fetching isn't built. | Start on staging; refresh the files when Sigstore rotates a shard. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#v2-rekor-v2-anchors-and-rfc-3161-timestamps) |
| macOS's system `openssl` (LibreSSL) can't verify the RFC 3161 timestamps. | It fails on their ESSCertIDv2 attribute. | Use OpenSSL 3 or `tracekit verify`. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#v2-rekor-v2-anchors-and-rfc-3161-timestamps) |
| A monitor sees the log the signer serves it: a split view shows only at equal tree sizes, or to witnesses. | A smaller checkpoint isn't proven consistent with the report's tree. | Run monitors and witnesses the operator doesn't control. | [server threat model](threat-model-server.md#known-limits) |
| A monitor the operator controls vouches for nothing, and a monitor report never raises `dev` or `local`. | Monitoring adds to independent witnessing, it doesn't replace it. | Run it somewhere the operator can't change; pin it as non-`operator`. | [monitor](monitor.md#verifying-with-it) |
| With an issuer, the monitor flags each `signer.epoch` after seq 0 as an unannounced key. | Rotating kids can't be listed in advance with `--allow`. | Expect those conflicts until the monitor can pin issuers. | [issuer](issuer.md#limits) |
| A Rekor entry the signer wrote but got no reply for shows as a monitor conflict. | It matches no stored anchor. | Check the signer's `witness_failed` gaps for that outage first. | [monitor](monitor.md#what-it-checks) |
| The issuer handles one issuance at a time and re-reads its log for each. | Fine at record-key volumes. | Past ~100k certificates it needs an index. | [issuer](issuer.md#limits) |
| If the issuer can't be reached at start, the signer doesn't start; records signed after a key's `not_after` fail verification. | Certificates are short-lived. | Keep the issuer available; watch renewals. | [issuer](issuer.md#the-signer) |
| The public witness makes a log `witnessed` independently of its operator, not of Cygnux. | Whoever holds the witness's key could cosign any tree. | Add a witness you or a partner run and raise `witnesses_required` in the trust config. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| `witnesses: [public]` and `init --v2 --public-witness` are refused until the public witness is deployed. | Its URL and vkey are set in `tracekit/public_witness.py` once it runs. | List your own witness, `{url, vkey, class}`, meanwhile. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| `init --public-witness` is for v2 system mode only. | The same-user dev signer has no signer.yaml. | Run a signer with a signer.yaml (`tracekit signer serve --config`) and list `public` there. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness takes one log key per origin, the first registered: a log under a new key is refused (403, `a new log key needs a new origin`). | Anyone can register, so the first key must be the only one, or anyone could take over a log's witnessing. | Set a new `origin` in signer.yaml when the log key changes. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| On the public witness the first registration of an origin wins: anyone can register a custom `origin` before its log does, and the log is then refused (`409`). | Registration is open; the witness can't tell who owns a name. | Keep the default origin `tracekit.local/<log id>` (unguessable before the log exists), or use your own witness. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness deletes an origin never cosigned within 24 hours of registering, or not cosigned for 90 days; the log registers again on its next checkpoint, and the witness's protection restarts from there. | A shared table must not fill with abandoned logs. | Checkpoint at least every 90 days, or use your own witness. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness tells anyone whether an origin is registered, and anyone holding an older checkpoint note of a log its current size. | `409` carries the size it holds, as the protocol says. | Treat a log's size as public once its notes are shared. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness takes only origins shaped `host/path` (lower-case host, at least one path segment); others are refused at registration (400, naming the format). | The origin names the log publicly, so it should name a host its operator controls. | Set `origin` in signer.yaml to e.g. `tracekit.example.com/log`. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness cosigns at most 30 checkpoints per log and client network a minute that grow the log; a busier log gets `429` and is cosigned at that rate, always its newest note. Resent and older notes are not counted. | It is a shared, free service. | Run your own witness for a higher rate. | [witnesses](witnesses.md#the-public-witness) |
| The public witness takes 100 registrations per client network (IPv4 /24 or IPv6 /48) a day, 60 in all a minute (`429`), and a fixed number of logs in total (then `this witness is full`). | Open registration must not let one client fill it. | Signers with many tenants register over several days (retries are automatic); when it is full, run your own witness. | [deploy/public-witness](../deploy/public-witness/README.md#limits) |

## Privacy

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| Commands, paths, URLs and search queries are recorded in clear (after redaction). | A reviewer needs to see what the agent acted on. | Deny sensitive patterns in policy, or keep bundles internal. | [privacy](privacy.md#what-hashes-and-metadata-still-leak) |
| Redaction is pattern-based and misses secrets it doesn't recognise. | It can only match known shapes. | Deny the pattern in policy, or keep bundles internal. | [privacy](privacy.md#redaction-patterns) |
| Browser Use's `sensitive_data` values are masked written out, percent-encoded, as HTML character references or JSON escapes, or a mix of these; any other transformation (base64, a hash, a split or a case change) reaches the signer as it is. | The adapter recognises a value by the forms a URL, a page or JSON writes it in. | Keep secrets out of URLs; deny a pattern that carries them in policy. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#sensitive_data) |
| v1 hashes are unsalted, so low-entropy content (a short prompt, a known file) can be confirmed by hashing guesses. | Reviewers can match known files. | Use the v2 signer, which publishes salted commitments. | [privacy](privacy.md#what-hashes-and-metadata-still-leak) |
| Sizes, timing and correlation still leak: the same file in two runs links them. | Hashes and commitments hide content, not activity. | Treat bundles as activity records. | [privacy](privacy.md#what-hashes-and-metadata-still-leak) |
| `content_capture: full` stores content (redacted) in the log and in bundles. | Readable content is what the setting is for. | Protect such bundles as you would the data. | [privacy](privacy.md#content-capture-hashed-or-full) |
| Revealing a v2 record's salt lets anyone holding the content check that record's commitments. | That is how an auditor checks content; one salt opens one record. | Reveal only the records an audit needs. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-v2-signer) |
| A run-set bundle carries the tenant's registry salt, so it allows testing guessed run ids against that tenant's leaves. | The verifier needs the salt to check completeness. | Share run-set bundles only with that tenant's auditors. | [auditor guide](auditor-guide.md#3-get-the-bundles) |
| With `serve_records: true` the metrics port serves every tenant's records (run ids, tool names, commitments). | The monitor reads every record. | Expose that port only to the monitor. | [monitor](monitor.md#run-it) |
| Witnesses learn when checkpoints happen and how many records exist; the public witness also learns each opted-in log's origin, so how often and how much each named log grows. | That is what they cosign, and it arrives when it is made. | Choose witnesses accordingly; for the public witness pick an `origin` that names nothing you want kept private, or don't opt in. | [privacy](privacy.md#what-hashes-and-metadata-still-leak), [public witness](privacy.md#what-leaves-the-machine) |

## Deployment

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A sidecar doesn't protect against whoever administers the node or the PVC. | They can reach the signer's volume. | A witness off the node detects rollback. | [deployment](deploy.md#container-and-kubernetes-sidecar) |
| **(1.2)** An identity authenticated by token, certificate or service account may call only the RPCs `authorize` grants it. The TypeScript and Python adapters need `register_run`, `decide`, `approval_consume`, `complete`, `close_run` and `model_event`; asks also need `approval_request` and `approval_wait`, and the OpenAI Agents JS and Vercel AI adapters `approval_list` (to apply decisions, and TypeScript's `instrument()` at exit to keep a run with waiting approvals open: without the grant it closes the run); `observe` and `state_write` where the app records inputs or state; `status` where the signer's content vault is on (without it the integrations keep what the model said as a digest only, and warn); and the coding-agent hooks on a remote signer (`tracekit init --remote ... --v2`) `observe` (each prompt) and `constraint_add` (live constraints). A missing grant refuses the call as `forbidden` (an ask is refused). | No identity has a grant by default. | Grant the agent identity that list; with the LLM gateway, the agent must register its runs as the identity the gateway relays. | [deployment](deploy.md), [gateway](gateway.md) |
| A KMS log key doesn't belong in the agent's pod. | Workload identity gives the pod's cloud credentials to every container, the agent's too. | Keep KMS keys on a central signer (`D-K8S-WORKLOAD-IDENTITY`). | [deployment](deploy.md#container-and-kubernetes-sidecar), [doctor](doctor.md#kubernetes-checks---k8s) |
| The sidecar chart runs one replica with the `Recreate` strategy. | The signer holds its data volume's storage lock. | Size the pod for the agent's load. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#sidecar) |
| `persistence.enabled: false` keeps the log in an emptyDir that dies with the pod. | It is for development. | Keep persistence on outside development. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#sidecar) |
| The sidecar's NetworkPolicy covers the whole pod, the agent's ports included. | NetworkPolicy selects pods, not containers. | Account for it when the agent serves ports. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#sidecar) |
| Central: scaling down closes the last replica's log for good, and that ordinal needs a new log before it runs again. | Each replica is the only writer of its own log. | Leave `autoscaling.scaleDown` off unless you plan for it. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#central) |
| Central: calls that name no run or approval reach one replica. | Runs are routed by their replica prefix. | List each replica's approvals through its own Service. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#central) |
| Central and compose don't protect against an operator with no independent witness and monitor, or against the cloud provider. | Detection needs both; the provider is out of scope. | Add both; keep witnesses on other parties' infrastructure. | [deployment](deploy.md#central), [server threat model](threat-model-server.md#s6-cloud-provider) |
| An agent-sandbox run records the agent's service account, not its Sandbox or pod: Sandboxes that share a ServiceAccount are one identity in the record. | The signer records the identity it authenticated, and the pod name isn't part of it. | Give each Sandbox its own ServiceAccount when runs must be told apart by Sandbox. | [agent-sandbox](integrations/agent-sandbox.md#what-the-bundle-shows-and-doesnt) |
| A Sandbox's RuntimeClass and spec aren't recorded: a run's `isolation: remote` says only that the signer runs apart from the agent, not how the agent's pod is isolated. | The signer sees an authenticated HTTPS caller, not how its pod runs. | Enforce the RuntimeClass in the cluster (an admission policy); don't read it from the bundle. | [agent-sandbox](integrations/agent-sandbox.md#what-the-bundle-shows-and-doesnt) |
| A run whose Sandbox is deleted before the agent closes it stays open until the signer's idle timeout closes it (`idle_s`, an hour by default); until then `tracekit verify` reads `VERIFIED TO HEAD n (open)`. | Only the agent or the idle timeout ends a run; the signer isn't told the pod is gone. | Export after the idle timeout for a closed run, or lower `idle_s`; the open run verifies up to its last record either way. | [agent-sandbox](integrations/agent-sandbox.md#run-it) |
| The agent-sandbox example's end-to-end test runs only by hand; CI doesn't run it. | It needs docker, kind, kubectl and the network (it installs agent-sandbox from its release). | Run `examples/v2/agent_sandbox/e2e.sh` before relying on a change to the example. | [agent-sandbox](integrations/agent-sandbox.md#run-it) |
| On compose, root on the Docker host is the operator. | It controls every container and volume. | Use an independent witness. | [deployment](deploy.md#compose) |
| **(1.2)** The failed-authentication limit of the HTTP services is per source address (4096 addresses remembered, least recently used dropped): a client that rotates through more addresses than that gets a fresh burst each time, and clients behind one NAT address share one limit, so one sending bad tokens can keep the others' OIDC and service-account tokens from being checked until it stops. | The limit protects the services that check those tokens (an IdP's keys, the API server's TokenReview); a client certificate or a Tracekit token is still checked locally. | Put the services behind a proxy that limits per client, or give clients behind one address client certificates or Tracekit tokens. | [deploy](deploy.md) |
| **(1.2)** The LLM gateway, the tool executor and the MCP gateway take the signer's connection limits (1024 in all, 256 per address), so one address can hold many slots with large headers before it is authenticated. | Agents behind one NAT address share an address; a lower fixed per-address limit would refuse them. | Put the services behind a proxy that limits connections per client. | [deploy](deploy.md) |
| **(1.5)** The loopback services take a fixed number of connections at once: the OTLP receiver (`tracekit otel serve`) 64, the proxy 16, `tracekit observe` 64 requests and 64 event streams; one more is closed unanswered. Each request must arrive within 30 s, and a connection idle for 10 s is closed. | Every local client has the one address, so the limit is the whole server's; without one, a local process could hold a thread per connection for as long as it liked. | Exporters and model clients retry a closed connection; send many exporters to the signer's OTLP import instead, and keep fewer observe pages open. | [OpenTelemetry](otel.md#delivery-guarantees) |
| **(1.2)** The MCP gateway answers `tools/list`, `resources/list`, `prompts/list` and `resources/read` from the upstreams before the signer has checked the run token (it is checked when the answer is recorded, which a forged token fails): an identity the gateway accepts can make it call every upstream once per request. | The listing is recorded as input the agent got, after it is fetched. | Accept only the agents' identities at the gateway, and rate-limit them in front of it. | [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md) |
| **(1.2)** Remote ingest charges one rate-limit token per OTLP request, however many spans it carries (up to 10,000), and keeps each client's OTLP state until the process restarts, a revoked client's too. | Requests are limited, not spans; client state is kept for its dedupe. | Restart `tracekit ingest serve` after revoking a client; keep exporters' batches modest. | [remote ingest](remote-ingest.md) |
| The compose stack's first start writes a `degraded_unanchored` gap. | The witness doesn't know the log yet. | Expected once; check later gaps. | [deploy compose](deploy-compose.md#the-witness) |
| The compose end-to-end test runs only where Docker and root (or passwordless sudo) are available; elsewhere it is skipped. | It brings up the real stack. | Run `deploy/compose/e2e.sh` on a Docker host before relying on a change. | [deploy compose](deploy-compose.md#end-to-end-test) |
| `tracekit doctor` output is advice about the host, not evidence. | Nothing it prints is signed or read by a verifier. | Rely on the bundle and the verifier's trust config. | [doctor](doctor.md) |
| A plain `http://` signer URL must name a loopback IP address (`http://127.0.0.1:8443`, `http://[::1]:8443`); `http://localhost` is refused, and a central signer's replica routing is not followed over it. | Over plain HTTP the bearer token crosses in clear: only an address, not a name a resolver could point elsewhere, keeps it on the host. | Use `127.0.0.1` or `::1`, or `https://`. | [deploy](deploy.md) |
| The public witness's compose file pins Caddy by tag, not digest. | The digest is taken when it is deployed. | Pin `caddy` by digest before production. | [deploy/public-witness](../deploy/public-witness/README.md) |
| The public witness's `state.db` must never roll back: a restored older copy could cosign a fork past it. | It is what keeps the witness from cosigning two trees. | A lost or rolled-back state means a new witness key and a tracekit release with the new vkey. | [deploy/public-witness](../deploy/public-witness/README.md#backup) |

## Investigation (`tracekit why`)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A measured effect is the total effect for this program on this task; it doesn't explain the model's reasons and may not transfer to other tasks. | Replay measures what changes when an input is removed, nothing more. | Re-test on the tasks you care about. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| `ruled-out` means smaller than 20 points, not zero. | Finite replays give an interval, not an exact value. | Raise `--n-max` until the interval is narrow enough for your decision. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| The viewer's single **RUN TEST** isn't corrected for testing many inputs one by one: about 1 in 20 inputs with no effect can come out `causal` by chance. | Each test reports its own interval. | Use **Find the cause** (`attribute`), which corrects for every test it runs. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| The causal graph is only as complete as the recorded context. | Content sent to a model without being recorded is invisible to it. | Record model calls through an adapter or the gateway. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| Replay costs model calls (about 2·n per test per downstream model call); program replay has no budgets or caching. | Proving a cause means re-running the model with and without each suspect. | Start with `attribute`, which tests the group first; set `--n-max`; on a signed run set `--max-calls` or `--max-cost`. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Run replay needs a re-executable program; decision replay doesn't. | Re-running a whole run means running its code again. | Use decision replay for calls recorded with their request. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| Tape matching is exact on tool arguments; new calls are stubbed, which can change what the agent does after the stub. | Replays must never repeat a real side effect. | Read `off_tape_actions` in each result. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| A tool your own code runs and records with `record_action` (or the Anthropic adapter's `tool_result`) runs for real in a replay, with a warning; only `act()` and the adapter's `run_tool` are served from the tape. | The runtime hears of such a call after it ran. | Run tools through `act()` or `run_tool`; read `off_tape_actions`. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| The CLI's replaying commands (`test`, `attribute`, `matrix`, `replay`) need `--system` or `--allow-program`; they never import the program a run names on their own. | Importing a module runs its code, and a run file is data anyone could have written. | Pass `--system module:ATTR` for code you trust. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| A run written to the folder by a local Runtime, or ingested before ingest tokens bound runs (no `ingest-owner` file), is never appended to by ingest. One token per run: an agent that changes token starts a new run. | A run is bound to the token whose batch created it. | Write each run through one sink, with one ingest token. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| The viewer keeps each analysed why run (its blobs included) in memory, and its event stream records, for the server's life. | The analysis is cached until a run's files change. | Restart the viewer, or move old runs out of the folder, when a workspace grows large. | [viewer](viewer.md#why) |
| Inferred edges compare each node with at most the 20 earlier nodes sharing the most word shingles with it, ignoring shingles found in more than 50 nodes, so a long run can miss some. | Comparing every pair is quadratic in the run's length. | Treat inferred edges as hints; observed and tested edges are complete. | [why](why.md#evidence-grades) |
| Analysing a run takes time that grows faster than its length: each sensitive call's alert searches everything upstream of it, so a run of 1,000 model calls that each see the whole history (an imported Claude Code session) takes about 25 s, and the viewer analyses a live run again each time it grows. | An alert names every untrusted input upstream of the call. | Open long runs once they finish, or record long sessions as several runs. | [why](why.md#alerts) |
| WHY's **how it got here** shows the chains from the 20 inputs nearest the call. | Every input's chain would grow with the cube of a long run's length. | **Candidate causes** lists every upstream input. | [viewer](viewer.md#why) |
| The viewer's GRAPH draws a run of up to 600 events; a larger one says it is too large to draw. | Thousands of nodes fit a panel only as dots too small to read, and laying them out takes seconds. | Use TIMELINE, WHY DID IT HAPPEN? or a call's WHY tab, or record long sessions as several runs. | [viewer](viewer.md#graph-click-a-node) |
| The viewer shows a run still being written up to the signer's newest checkpoint and checks it again no sooner than four times the CPU time its last check took; its graph is imported again on the same terms (the analysis of each import, about as long again, is not counted), and a final run's findings come after its verdict. A check costs about 0.5 ms for each record it has not checked before and 0.05 ms for each one it has, so a 25,000-call run (50,000 records) shows its newest calls 10 to 30 s late (it was 30 to 110 s, with one CPU busy). | A record is shown only once a checkpoint covers it and the run's bundle up to there verifies; checking every run in full at each checkpoint kept one CPU busy and left the page minutes behind. | Wait; `tracekit verify` on an exported bundle covers the same checkpointed records. | [viewer](viewer.md#runs-and-the-tape) |
| While it checks and imports a run being written the viewer holds that run's records in memory a few times over: next to a 25,000-call run it used about 1 to 2 GB. | Each check exports and verifies the run's bundle, and the graph imports the run whole. | Give the viewer that memory, or view a long run once it is final (`tracekit export --v2 --run ID`, then `tracekit verify`). | [viewer](viewer.md#runs-and-the-tape) |
| Past `TRACEKIT_OBSERVE_MAX_RECORDS` rows (50,000) the viewer and its page drop the oldest from the live view, and the tape says how many left; each run's latest verdict stays in the run list, for up to 10,000 runs (a failed run's goes last). | The live view is held in memory. | Export a run's bundle (`tracekit export --v2 --run ID`) for all its records, or raise `TRACEKIT_OBSERVE_MAX_RECORDS`. | [viewer](viewer.md#runs-and-the-tape) |
| PERMISSIONS lists each decision that was not a plain allow from the rows the page holds: with no run selected, those of every run in the live view (the newest `TRACEKIT_OBSERVE_MAX_RECORDS` rows, 50,000 by default); with a run selected, that run's rows, read again from the store (`/api/rows`) when the run verified and some of its records left the live view. A run that did not verify, or is still being checked, lists only its decisions still in the live view. While it lists none for the selected run (its rows still loading, or none left on the page) it shows the run review's `permissions` instead: a count per outcome, not each decision. | The review of every run stays in memory and is in each snapshot, so it holds counts, not decisions, whatever the run's size; rows are read from the store only for a run whose bundle verifies. | Select the run, or export its bundle (`tracekit export --v2 --run ID`, then `tracekit verify`). | [viewer](viewer.md#pages-and-a-runs-views) |
| A run read again from the store (`/api/rows`) shows at most `TRACEKIT_OBSERVE_MAX_RECORDS` of its records, its first, without the arguments the dev signer kept; its answer is kept while the run's own records don't grow, so an edit of its records shows only once the viewer's own re-check fails the run (within five minutes). The viewer answers from its own watcher's state and the answers it kept, with no read of the store: a run the watcher hasn't verified yet is answered 404 until it has. Only a check reads the store, two at once at most: a third request that needs one is answered 503 and the page tries again 10 s later; the page keeps the rows of the four runs read last. | Reading kept arguments again writes a signed content-access record each time; checking the whole run on every request would keep a CPU busy, and opening the store reads every record of every run. | Wait, or export the run's bundle for all its records; for the arguments, `tracekit signer reveal`. | [viewer](viewer.md#laptop-viewer-api) |
| The central viewer's run list (`/api/runs`) shows each run's last verdict, up to five minutes old: lists check one run at a time, with a check slot of their own (so a run page always has one), and a list that finds it taken shows FAILED as it is and NOT CHECKED for a run it has never checked or whose VERIFIED is older than that. An edit of a run's records in place shows in the list at the first list after that that can check it, and at once on the run's own page (`/api/run`), which checks it for each request, two at once at most with bundle downloads (`/api/bundle`; a third is answered 503: try again). | Checking every listed run on every list would export and verify each one each time; each check reads the log. | Open the run's page, or export its bundle and run `tracekit verify`. | [viewer](viewer.md#central-viewer) |
| HORIZON's project ring holds a v2 run's relative paths (an absolute path is "this machine"), places a call whose arguments the viewer has only as a commitment by its tool, draws the newest 500 calls and sensor effects of all runs together by the signer's time, or a why log's own (not 500 per run; the page says how many it leaves out), draws a why log time it can't read as the oldest (its tape row shows it as now, and its replay clock reads 00:00:00), and shows network effects a kernel sensor saw but not the egress observer's. | `run.registered` records no working directory; the egress observer's records reach the page without a structured effect; a why log on disk isn't checked when it loads. | Open a run's CALLS for every call and its record; check a place with the run's bundle. | [Event Horizon](horizon.md#limits) |
| A run the viewer already showed is checked again five minutes after its last check, one run a second, so an edit of records it showed turns the run `FAILED` (and takes them back) within minutes, later with many runs. | Checking every run at every store change would check the whole store each second. | For evidence run `tracekit verify` on an exported bundle; restart the viewer to check every run at once. | [viewer](viewer.md#runs-and-the-tape) |
| `verify` reports a log without `run.end` (cut short, or still recording) and test results outside `tests.jsonl`'s chain; whoever can rewrite the files can rebuild both chains, a closing `run.end` included, and the why log's head is not yet recorded in the signed records. | Both chains are unsigned. | Cite the signed records for claims; keep the why folder where agents cannot write. | [why](why.md#limits) |
| A webhook gets an alert's rule, title and run only, and at most 100 alerts wait to be sent; more are dropped with a warning. | Alerts must not carry the call's arguments, nor stall the server behind a slow endpoint. | Open the run in the viewer for the detail. | [why](why.md#live-mode-and-the-guard) |
| LangGraph fork replay serves tool calls only through a `ToolNode` that is a node of the graph (also inside `.with_fallbacks()`) or of a subgraph added as a node. A graph without one is refused ("the graph has no ToolNode ..."), and so is a node that runs a subgraph with a ToolNode from its own code. A tool called any other way (a ToolNode invoked inside a function node, a tool called inside a model node) runs for real: a branch with a tool result that was not served stops with an error ("... ran outside a ToolNode ..."), but only after that tool ran. A call a node of the app answers itself (a routing schema such as `CompleteOrEscalate`) is refused the same way unless `routing=` (`--routing`) names it, and a call so named is never checked. | ToolNode exposes the hook replay serves from; code inside a node can call anything. | Put your tools in a `ToolNode` node (`create_agent` and `tracekit_tool_node` do); add subgraphs as nodes; name only routing schemas in `routing=`. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| A tool call is served with its recorded `ToolMessage` (content, status, artifact). A thread in which a tool step also wrote another state key (a tool returning `Command(update=...)`) is refused ("a tool step of this thread also wrote ..."); a `Command`'s `goto` isn't replayed. | Replay serves results from the thread; what else a tool's `Command` did isn't a message it can replay. | Have tools return messages; keep other state in nodes. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Fork replay removes messages only: a suspect whose step also wrote another state key is refused ("the step that added ... also wrote ..."). | Content that entered through another key would stay in the branch, so removing the message would wrongly rule it out. | Keep what a node adds to the state in `messages`, or test another suspect. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Branch nodes see a scratch thread: a new `thread_id` per branch, an empty scratch store if the graph has one, and no node cache. A node that reads long-term memory from the store sees none of it in either arm. | Branches must not read or write the app's thread, store or cache, and a cache would serve every unaltered branch the recorded model output. | Read the effect as measured without the store's contents; key external lookups on the state, not the thread_id. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Fork replay runs graphs synchronously: an async-only node or an async checkpointer is refused ("... is async-only ...", "... checkpointer is async ..."), and so is a subgraph with its own checkpointer. | Branches are driven with `invoke`; an async saver is bound to its own event loop. | Give async nodes a sync function too, and give the factory the sync checkpointer over the same database. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Fork replay replaces a removed tool result with a placeholder instead of deleting the message. | Model APIs reject a tool call left without its result. | Read the effect as "without that content", which is what the placeholder gives. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| An attribution forks once, where the earliest suspect entered; a later suspect that isn't a tool result is refused ("... entered after the fork point ..."). | Every test shares one unaltered arm, so they must fork at the same point. | Test that message on its own (`fork_replay`, or `--suspect` with only it). | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Fork replay refuses a suspect in the thread's first checkpoint or added by a step that ran several different nodes at once ("cannot tell which node added ..."); parallel calls of one node (a ToolNode running several tool calls) are fine. | It forks with `update_state(as_node=...)`, which needs the one node that wrote the message. | Pick another suspect, or run those nodes one after the other. | [why](why.md#how-causality-is-tested) |
| Inferred edges are a heuristic and miss paraphrase. | They come from word overlap, not a recorded path. | Treat them as hints; only tested edges say "caused". | [why](why.md#evidence-grades) |
| The `tracekit why` guard is a string-matching tripwire, in the agent's process. | It checks verbatim values only, where the agent runs. | Use the signer's `from: untrusted` rules, which the agent can't skip. | [why](why.md#live-mode-and-the-guard) |
| `tracekit why`'s own log is hash-chained but unsigned: whoever can rewrite the whole log can rebuild the chain. | It holds the content a signer never stores. | Cite the signed Tracekit records for every claim that matters. | [why](why.md) |
| With a signer, a message to another agent is observed in the recipient's run as one value with the message's trust: a value the sender had from trusted input but passed on in an untrusted message is untrusted there, and a `from: untrusted` rule holds it. | The signer's provenance index is per run and sees what the agent was sent, not where each part came from. | Put the values your app supplies in the task, which every agent's run observes as trusted, or answer the hold. | [why](why.md#with-the-signer) |
| **(1.2)** A handoff to another agent isn't decided: the model asking for one is a `reconcile.hook_missing` (its detail says what to do) unless the policy's `handoffs:` names it; no shipped pack names any. A tool the policy names that way isn't flagged when it runs without a decide, even under `gateway_mandatory`, and a child policy can't remove a name its parent declares. | Frameworks run handoffs themselves, without a call id an adapter could gate; the app chooses its tools' names. | List your agents' handoff tools by exact name (`transfer_to_refunds_agent`, LangGraph routing schemas such as `CompleteOrEscalate`: [LangGraph](quickstarts/langchain.md#routing-between-assistants-handoffs)); a loose glob is refused. | [policy](policy-v2.md#file-format) |
| A tool call your program makes without `decision=` shows in the bundle as `reconcile.fabricated` (no model asked for it). | Reconciliation compares each decide with the tool calls of the run's model exchanges. | Pass `decision=` when a model asked for the call; read the others as calls of the program. | [why](why.md#with-the-signer) |
| With a signer, each model call's response record (the tool calls it led to) is written when the run finishes, after the run's other records. | Those tool calls are known only after they ran. | Read the request record for when the call was made. | [why](why.md#with-the-signer) |
